About the Town
SETTLED:
1718
INCORPORATED:
August 1, 1728
TYPE OF GOVERNMENT:
Town Meeting
LOCATION:
North Central Massachusetts, bordered by Townsend on
the north, Shirley on the east, Leominster and Lancaster
on the south, and Fitchburg and Ashby on the west. It
is about 47 miles from Boston and 29 miles from Worcester.
COUNTY:
Worcester
ELEVATION AT TOWN HALL:
Approximately 570 ft. above mean sea level
LAND AREA:
26.63 Square Miles
TOTAL AREA:
29 Square Miles
TOWN ROADS:
76.97 Miles

===PAGE 6===
Board of Selectmen
SHERWOOD G. WINCHESTER
RUDOLPH MASELLA
NORMAND THOMPS
Board of Selectmen: left to right - Sherwood G. Winchester, Chairman Rudolph Masella, and Normand J. Thompson.
It is the privilege of the Board of Selectmen to bring to the attention of the residents of Lunenburg certain
events which have taken place during 1974--events which affect not only the year 1974, but also many years to
come, These events were distinguished because they were deliberations by groups of interested citizens in addition
to deliberations by official Town Committees. This voluntary participation of interested citizens in addition to the
official Committees is a most healthy development and should be encouraged. It is only through concerned action
by all people that Lunenburg can have a healthy, progressive and constructive growth.
It is unnecessary to list in detail the events of the past year on a day to day basis, and even to list capital
projects, as these. are all covered by the reports of the many Town Committees and Town Department Heads elsewhere
in this Report.
However, there is one thing that should be underlined, and that is the growing cooperation between the various
Town Committees. This kind of cooperation can lead to the elimination of duplication of efforts with an ultimate
savings of Town funds. The Board of Selectmen suggests that in reading the 1974 Town Report, one carefully notes
the cooperation of Committees. The Board suggests that in 1975 a series of workshops be set up where all elected and
appointed officials can meet to mutually discuss their obligations and responsibilities. Such study groups could con-
tribute greatly to efficiency in government.
As more services are demanded to meet the modern concepts of a progressive and expanding community, costs
must inevitably rise. The Board of Selectmen feels that the rising costs for all services in all departments must be
given their rightful priority. With each passing year the operation of government requires greater effort on the part
of all charged with public responsibility.
As Selectmen, we can certainly utilize constructive thoughts and suggestions to aid and assist this office in the
proper promotion of our community. It has always been and remains the desire of the Board of Selectmen to improve
all local functions in order to give our citizens the best possible services at the least possible cost.
-4-
===PAGE 7===
Board of Health
As a member of the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, the Town of Lunenburg receives professional health
services to meet the requirements of the State Sanitary Code and the Regulations of the local Board of Health. The
Nashoba Associated Boards of Health also provides in-service educational programs and consultation for our Town
Nursing staff including such social work consultation as may be required. The sixteen member towns of the Nashoba
District represent over 74,000 people. The following is a resume of health services received during 1974.
Environmental Sanitation

301
Conferences with Board of Health members and State Officials by Nashoba personnel
7
Dental Health - The dental health program consisted of inspection of 156 students in the first grade and 394 in the
second, fifth and eighth grades.
Rabies Clinic - All sixteen member towns of the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health participated in the Rabies
Clinics and a total of 2,153 dogs were inoculated of which 216 were in Lunenburg.

===PAGE 8===
Board of Assessors
The Board of Assessors in line with our policy of maintaining the program established by the revaluation of
1970, has continued to annually view each parcel of property in Town. In view of the decision of the State Supreme
Court that all property in the Commonwealth will be valued at 100% of full and fair market value, the Assessors can,
using a percentage multiplier, raise or lower the valuations of all property in the Town to reflect the affects of in-
flation or recession on the value of property. Contrary to the views expressed in several recent newspaper articles
that enforcement of this decision will cost the taxpayers a huge sum of tax dollars to upgrade, your Assessors wish
to assure the voters that this process can be completed by the existing staff at no additional cost to the Town.
The total value of Real Estate has increased to $52,009,970.00 in 1974 as compared to $51,014,700.00 in
1973. Personal property valuation increased to $2,908,970.00 in 1974 as compared to $2,712, 180.00 in 1973.
We find the increases are due to new construction, additions and conversion of summer cottages to year-round homes
in the lake areas. The Board was pleased to announce the $44.00 tax rate for Fiscal Year 1975 and feels that this
reduction of $7.00 demonstrates that an effective effort by all the voters can hold down expenditures in an infla-
tionary economy.
Mrs. Ine 1. Pihlaja was hired to do the clerical work for the Assessors' Office filling the vacancy created by
the resignation of Mrs. Joan E. Souliere. Mrs. Pihlaja formerly worked in the Tax Collector's Office for fourteen
years. Mrs. Gloria J. Knight, a former part-time employee in the Assessors' Office, was hired to work full-time
under the Emergency Employment Act at no cost to the Town.
The clerical staff has had considerable new and additional work this year, including coding of all property as
to residential, forest, agricultural, commercial or industrial status in order to start the Chapter 61A Agricultural
Land Program. Under this program, agricultural or horticultural land can be so designated, if qualified, thus re-
sulting in considerable savings. This program, while very beneficial to the career farmer or fruit grower, involves
severe penalties for selling land for other purposes during the first ten years of the program.
The Board of Assessors wishes to remind taxpayers of certain exemptions that have been enacted concerning
the value of residential property, owned and occupied as a domicile by persons in various groups for whom the pay-
ment of taxes could constitute an undue burden. These exemptions may not exceed certain specified amounts. If
a taxpayer feels that he qualifies for any of the following exemptions, he should contact the Assessors' Office and
request an application which lists all the requirements that must be met in order to qualify.
Section 17 - Widows, minors whose fathers are deceased, and persons over 70 - $2,000 in valuation or a tax of
$175, whichever is greater.
Section 22 - Disabled or wounded veterans, or their wives, widows or parents $2,000 to $10,000 or a tax from
$175 to $875, whichever is greater, depending on degree of disability.
Section 37 - Blind persons - $5,000 valuation or a tax of $437.50, whichever is greater.
Section 41 - Persons aged 70 or over - $4,000 valuation or a tax of $350, whichever is greater.
Section 42 - Unmarried widows of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty $8,000 valuation or a
tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Section 43 - Minor orphans of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 in valuation or a
tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Tax deferral for certain persons 65 years of age or over. (This isa new addition to the law whichallows
Chapter 59, a property owner over 65 years of age with gross receipts of under $20,000 annually to place the taxes
Section 5 on his domicile and one acre of land on a deferred payment plan, the taxes to be paid upon settlement
Clause 41A of the estate plus 8% interest.)
The Board of Assessors wishes to reiterate that the Board and the clerical staff are available to any taxpayer
requiring assistance or information. We wish to thank the citizens of Lunenburg for the cooperation shown to US
throughout the year.

===PAGE 10===
Park Commission
The Summer Recreation Program ran very smoothly under the leadership of our very able Director, Richard
Stachowicz. The Muscular Dystrophy Fair netted more money than ever before-$174.00, thanks to our two fine
Playground Supervisors, Gail Okerman and Barbara Gervais. Youngsters spent a number of hours working on Arts
and Crafts under the careful tutelage of Kimberly Craffey and Stephanie Markham. Some of the highlights were an
inter-playground bike race, a trip to the Alice G. Wallace Planetarium, the Cinema, picnic-swim at Willard Brook,
a trip to Benson's Animal Farm, bubble gum and watermelon hunts, etc.
We also support a number of other programs such as the High School Basketball League, Junior League Street
Hockey and Basketball under the fine supervision of James Harley and Alan Cartwright, Morning League Baseball
with Steven Esposito and Alan Cartwright handling the reins, Men's Softball League and Girl's Softball under the
skillful handling of Chuck Winneberg.
Mr. John Therrien conducteda very successful Tennis Program this past summer. The adult group for beginners
only was very well attended. The program concentrated on basic fundamentals and technique. John cited the need
for additional courts, for increasing the adult program to two nights a week, and for lessons at Wallis Park in the
Whalom area.
The Town Beach under the experienced and capable direction of Gerald Beloin was a beehive of activity.
The lifeguards as usual were very dependable and always "on the alert". Robert Clarke initiated Water Polo this
past summer. It is hoped that with advanced publicity this program will be more successful next summer. The Swim
Program reached a number of youngsters--over 500 to be exact. This program includes life saving also. The Tiny
Tot Program (24-36 months old) and the Adult Swim Program were very worthwhile. June Riddle is in charge of the
Swim Program and is a real asset to the Town. The Lunenburg Red Cross has assisted with these programs for a number
of years.
We are very proud of the appearance of all of our facilities thanks to the dedicated supervision of Robert
Garneauand the very capable assistance of Andre Garneau and Richard Harper. These people save the Townspeople
a lot of tax dollars each year.
Anumber of projects have been completed this past year. The chain link fence was completed at Lesure Field.
Concrete slabs have been poured for the baseball team benches at Marshall Park. The back-stop at Marshall Park
was painted as was the fence at Wallis Park. The undergrowth at Lesure Field was cleared out, greatly improving
the appearance of this facility. The "Quonset Hut" at Wallis Park has been demolished and the retaining wall at
the Town Beach was not completed because of insufficient funds. We plan to supplement this with $3,000.00.
We also help in a number of other programs such as: Pop Warner Football, Junior Basketball, Open-Gym in
the winter, Teen-Age Basketball, Young Men's Basketball, skating at Marshall Pond, AAU Track Meets, Men's
Golf League and Couples' Scotch Foursome Golf League.
There are several projects "in the wind" for another year. We needa new tractor for mowing and other chores.
Additional tennis courts are badly needed and it is hoped that four such courts can be constructed on land adjacent
to the High School parking lot. In a period of austerity and limited travel it is hoped that more Townspeople will
turn to a more localized form of recreation. There is a need for a good softball field and one or two more baseball
fields. All of these projects depend upon your support. If you feel that they are worthwhile, please give US your
support.
-8-
===PAGE 11===
Town Clerk, Tax Collector, Treasurer
.....
Mary L. Padula
Any report on the conversion by all of the Cities and Towns in the Commonwealth from a calendar year to a
fiscal year would most certainly contain SOME GOOD NEWS and SOME BAD NEWS.
In Lunenburg, the GOOD NEWS is - that in 1974 we did not have to borrow in anticipation of revenue (in
1973 we borrowed $1,050,000 with interest totalling $18,804); that we earned $55,668 worth of interest on Rev-
enue Cash Invested (in 1973 we earned only $6,372 or $12,432 less than interest paid out); that the net effect of
the difference in terms of savings on next year's tax rate will be $1.20; and that, in general, property owners seem
to favor paying their real estate taxes on a semi-annual basis.
The BAD NEWS is that although raccounting is now on a fiscal basis, the Town Report is still on an annual
one; that there is considerably more processing and bookkeeping involved in the double billing system; that record
property owners as of the previous January 1 must be billed for the tax year commencing in July and ending in June;
that, as a result, the benefits of abatements granted to such record owners are more apt to inure to the benefit of the
wrong person-the new owners; and that for this reason and because thebills themselves are complicated, taxpayers
are finding the double billing confusing and difficult to understand. Bills are now being filed with the Legislature
which would allow for two distinctly separate real estate tax bills per year.

Motor Vehicle Excise billing continues to be a source of annoyance to Taxpayers and Tax Officials alike. An
examination of excise bills issued by the State Department of Motor Vehicles has shown that approximately 25% of
themare in error. The resulting confusion and inconvenience to vehicle owners and to the municipality which processes
these bills is immeasurable, as is the cost involved. City and Town officials throughout the State are calling for a
complete revision and correction of the system presently employed by the Registry of Motor Vehicles.
A particular problem with the Excise exists in the Town of Lunenburg due to the fact that a large percentage of
our residents have a Fitchburg mailing address. This means that the forms completed when a vehicle is registered show
Lunenburg as the placeof principal garaging but also bear a Fitchburg address. All too often the Registry carelessly
processes such bills to Fitchburg and the Fitchburg Board of Assessors and/or Tax Collector don't get too upset about
collecting the money which should rightfully be offsetting Lunenburg's Real Estate Tax Rate. If you should receive
a bill from the City of Fitchburg on a vehicle which is garaged in Lunenburg--DON'T PAY IT! Instead, contact the
Fitchburg Board of Assessors, advise them that you live and garage your car in Lunenburg and request that the Fitch-
burg bill be abated and sent to Lunenburg. You will benefit in two ways. The excise paid to Lunenburg will save
you money by offsetting the amounts which would otherwise be raised in Real Estate Taxes, and you will have longer
to pay the bill as it will not be overdue until 60 days after it is reissued by the Town of Lunenburg. The $302,792
in Motor Vehicle and Trailer Excise collected in calendar 1973 reduced the 1975 fiscal year tax rate by $5.50 per
thousand
The Federal Revenue Sharing Program, which only was promised for five years or through
What
Fiscal 1977, is facing amendments in 1975. Most local officials feel Revenue Sharing is the
greatest program they have ever had, as there are few strings attached. Included in the
will be

years were special construction projects on Howard Street and Reservoir Road, the
the Fate of

way Department, extensive corrections of drainage problems in streets throughout the Town,
Revenue
purchase of radio and communications equipment for the Police and Fire Departments, and
recreational facilities and equipment. An additional $130,000 is being allocated for the
Sharing?

Planning Board
Some of the major items on the year's agenda included work on subdivisions, lots not requiring subdivision ap-
proval, a court case, further work on the sewer system, flood plain insurance study, and finally a complete reorgan-
ization of our maps and data filing, which was done by in large, by the Board's new filing clerk.
The Board was involved with five subdivisions in 1974. One new subdivision, Heritage Hill off West and
Pleasant Streets, containing some 198 one-half acre lots was finally approved. It had been submitted in 1972 at
which time one-half acre lots were allowed under the Protective By-law. The second phase of the "Broadmeadow"
subdivision continued to be developed.
Two new subdivisions have been submitted--one "Laurelwood Realty Trust" on Chase Road was submitted as a
preliminary plan and contains nine lots. The final Definitive Plan has not as yet been approved. The other new
subdivision is called "Prescott Place" and is located off Leominster Road north of Kilburn Street and contains fifteen
lots all of which, according to plans, will contain two-family rental homes. A final Definitive Plan was submitted
in early December, but upon advice of the Planning Board, the owner decided to recall it in its entirety and plans
to resubmit in the future.
The last subdivision, which involved the court case, was Whalom Estates. It is located on West Street and
Hollis Road. The plan which was approved in 1960 contained 195 lots, many of which were less than one-half acre
in size. Twenty-nine lots were developed some years ago. The Board, upon reviewing the remaining subdivision,
recommended that it be upgraded to current zoning and subdivision by-law standards and, consequently, rescinded
the 1960 approval. Subsequently the Town was taken to courtand the court held that the subdivision, before further
development, must be brought up to the current standards as defined in the Protective By-law.
LOTS NOT REQUIRING SUBDIVISION APPROVAL
This year the Board processed 63 lots in this category. This was a decrease over 1973 of 7, an increase over 1972
of 19, an increase over 1971 of 29, and an increase over 1970 of 51. Of the 63 processed this year, 28 were in the
Watershed Zone.
LOTS REQUIRING SUBDIVISION APPROVAL
As noted earlier in the report, 198 new lots requiring subdivision approval were processed. This brings the total
number of new lots in 1974 to 261, an increase of 191 over 1973, 217 over 1972, 227 over 1971, and 249 over 1970.

Tree Warden
Because of a high priority rating by the U.S. Department
of Agriculture and the Department of Natural Resources involved
in a Gypsy Moth research project, the Massachusetts Department
of Natural Resources' work of removal and scouting assistance
regularly performed in Lunenburg was seriously curtailed.
One hundred and seventy diseased trees were cut down,
chipped and buried. The Department of Natural Resources gave
four days of tree removal work, amounting to $400.00. The Town
was reimbursed $444.03 by the County. A total of $3,047.49
was spent on this work, added to the $9,045.46 expended for
calendar year 1973, makes a total of $12,092.95 spent for the
transitional 18-month period ending June 30, 1974. A total of
$5,590.39 was spent on this work in the last six months of 1974
under the Dutch Elm Account.
Forty-two trees were purchased and planted on Leominster
Road and on the Common in Lunenburg Center.
Richard W. Brown, Tree Warden
Work under the Tree Warden Department cost $1,569.70,
added to the $5,756.30 expended for calendar year 1973, makes a total of $7,326.00 spent for the transitional 18-
month period ending June 30, 1974. A total of $2,746.53 was spent in the last six months of 1974.

===PAGE 16===
Library Trustees
Lunenburg is using its Libraryl As usual, the books, magazines, records and films circulated at a very pro-
lific rate over the past year. Adult books went out 17,658 times, children's books 23, ,094, magazines 214, and
recordings 424. Interlibrary loans numbered 173, and 104 films were lent. The amazing thing, though, is that peo-
ple are beginning to view the Library as much more than just a book place. They now see it as a place to learn,
no matter what the means. They meet here to discuss such diverse topics as drugs, conservation, crafts and tran-
scendentalism. They come to gaze at art exhibits, to join in craft displays, to enjoy a sing-along or an afternoon
tea, or to enjoy a movie they might not get the opportunity to see elsewhere.
Much of the credit for this lively interest in the Ritter Memorial Library is due to the efforts of the "Friends
of the Library", both for last year's revitalization of the meeting room and for their planning of many stimulating
events. Upcoming is a series of "interest" lectures and demonstrations by authorities in many fields.
Along with regular activities, we hope in the coming year to update our holdings in important areas. Also,
we plan to prepare interesting Bicentennial displays and to increase information on learning disabilities. We also
hope to reach people who for a number of reasons cannot get to the Library, and to obtain some audio-visual equip-
ment for those who cannot attain the information they need in regular book form.
Of the programs already existing, some are noteworthy because of their great success. The Children's Story
Hours have enjoyed nearly perfect attendance and enthusiasm by both the children and their parents. The films shown
at the Library have attracted consistently good turnouts. The Film Society, sponsored by the Library, has done an
outstanding job on both the selection and promotion of first-rate films, with people from outside this immediate lo-
cale remarking on the fine offerings.
All in all, things are certainly happening at the Library. There is a new Librarian and Assistant Librarian,
who along with the rest of the staff, hope that the residents of Lunenburg will continue to support, use and enjoy
the wealth of information and entertainment they can find at the Ritter Memorial Library.
Library Trustees - left to right - Janet J. Cragin, Barbara A. Murray, Carey D. Rhoten and Librarian John E.
Ramsay. Absent when picture was taken were Beverly A. Mulholland, Robert S. Tapply and John 1. Burgess.

===PAGE 17===
Housing Authority
In March 1974 the Lunenburg Housing Authority hired the accounting firm of John S. Sullivan which deals
exclusively with Housing Authorities. Their expertise in dealing with the accounting procedures of the Massachu-
setts Department of Community Affairs, under which we operate, has proved invaluable.
With the approval of the Commonwealth, changes in age and income eligibility requirements have been ad-
opted. Persons 62 years of age are now eligible, and maximum income requirements have been raised to $4200.00
for a single person and to $4800.00 for a couple.
An active social program is available to those who wish to participate. Whist parties, pot luck suppers,
speakers, bus trips, vegetable gardens, the Knit 'n Stitch Club, woodworking -- there is something for everyone
The Pearl Brook Tenants' Association acts as an active liaison between tenants and the Authority. We encourage
interested Lunenburg residents to call Mrs. Alleva at the Authority Office to inquire further, as all vacancies are
filled from the list of preliminary applications on file.
We would like to publicly thank the Lunenburg Memorial Fund for the screen house which makes it possible
to sit outdoors on those long hot summer evenings when the mosquitos buzz and buzz and buzzzzzz.
Subsidies from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts pay for all debt service and have reduced the original de-
velopment cost of $809,000.00 to a current debt of $776,000.00. During a period of high taxes and much dissatis-
faction with government services, Pearl Brook Apartments stands out as one area where tax money is well spent and
does accomplish its intent - providing the means for many of our elderly citizens to live productive lives with dignity.
Lunenburg Housing Authority - seated left to right - Secretary Eileen M. Alleva,
Chairman Ann P. Hall, and Josephine C. Meloon. Standing left to right - Arthur J.
Brockelman, Howard O. Russell, Chairman Council on Aging, and Richard 1. White.

Council on Aging
During 1974 regular monthly meetings of the Council on Aging were held at the Recreation Building at the
Pearl Brook Apartments. Present during our meeting in May was Mr. Robert Arsenault, Director of the nutrition
program for this area. Mr. Arsenault was able to answer our questions relative to hot meals and transportation-the
two subjects which are of primary concern to US.
Members of our Council have attended area meetings in Leominster and Fitchburg which have been most in-
formative. In July a bus trip for over forty elderly went to Freedom Acres. On December 12, 1974, one hundred
and twenty senior citizens and invited guests attended our second annual catered Christmas Buffet, which was held
at the United Parish with an outstanding music program by the Jr. - Sr. High School. The following is an excerpt
of the By-laws of the Town of Lunenburg:
ARTICLE XVIII - COUNCIL ON AGING - There is hereby established a local Council on Aging con-
sisting of not less than seven (7) nor more than eleven (11) voting members. Of this number, the Chair-
man, and not less than three (3) nor more than five (5) persons, shall be appointed by the Board of Se-
lectmen from the registered voters of the Town of Lunenburg. The Chairman of the Council, when ap-
pointed, shall designate the remaining members of the Council from interested and representative groups
in the Community. The local Council on Aging shall be responsible to the Board of Selectmen, and its
members shall serve without compensation, and within the limits of available funds. It may appoint such
clerks and other employees as it may require. It shall be the duty of the local Council on Aging to carry
out programs designed to meet the problems of the aging in coordination with programs of the Commission
on Aging established under General Laws, Chapter 6, Section 73, and to submit an annual report to the
Town and a copy thereof to said Commission.

Personnel Committee
The Personnel Committee is carrying
out its work under increasingly restrictive
circumstances as more laws are passed on
Beacon Hill and in our Nation's Capitol.
Minimumwage laws now apply to mu-
nicipalities, union negotiation arbitration
procedures are dictated, and some employee
wages are now determined by law. Add to
this an inflation rate of close to 12%, in-
creasing unemployment, a recession and you
arrive at a formula for which there may be
no solution.
Nevertheless, the Personnel Com-
mittee, with the patient understanding of
many Town employees, has arrived at so-
lutions which it hopes represent a fair ba-
lance between the needs of the taxpayers
and those who provide the many services re-
quired by the Town.
The Selectmen and the Moderator, in
their appointments to the Committee, have
chosen people with widely varying back-
grounds. This has provided the Committee
Personnel Committee - Seated: Ellen Kemp and Chairman
with broad experience from which to draw
William 1. Clapp. Standing left to right - Robert D. Clarke,
on when considering the many problems in
Robert H. Walker and Herman A. Peed.
Personnel Administration which arise dur-
ing the year.
The present Committee includes a Business Owner, a Director of Purchasing and Personnel, a Multi-Plant
Manager for a major company, a Technical Services Manager and a Supervising Nurse.
The Committee has continued its familiar pattern of studying Personnel Plans of other communities, industry
and business wage and benefit levels, and has conducted reviews with Town Department Heads. Information drawn
from these sources provides the background for reaching decisions for employees of the Town and for revisions to the
Salary Administration Plan.
The following is an excerpt from Section 1 of the Salary Administration Plan: "The Personnel Committee may
from time to time issue, amend, or revoke Administrative Orders for the purpose of giving effect to the provisions
of this by-law, and for the purpose of giving effect to the provisions of such other by-laws and votes of the Town,
pertaining to personnel administration, as said committee may be responsible for administering and/or enforcing.
Each such order shall be numbered in sequence, and said committee shall maintain in the Town Office a file for all
such orders issued, said file to be open to public inspection."

===PAGE 20===
250th Anniversary Committee
The Committee to plan the 250th Anniversary of the Town of Lunenburg was expanded to seven members in
1974, and all are involved in planning an observance of the Town's 250th Anniversary which will occur in 1978.
The actual date of incorporation as a Town was August 1, 1728. As it looks now, not only will this date be ob-
served in some fashion, but a series of events will be held during 1978.
The calendar for the observance is in the process of being finalized. Plans include a Kick-Off Dance com-
bined with a Winter Carnival in the opening months of 1978, a Celebration Week sometime in the late summer, and
a Ball to be held after Labor Day, The Lunenburg Rotary Club has undertaken the sponsorship of the Ball, and it is
hoped that all Town organizations will follow suit to conduct some of the activities.
The Historical Society is working actively on the writing of Lunenburg's History which is to be published.
From this, a pageant will be staged and will be presented sometime during the 250th Program. If interest warrants,
both a foot and mounted militia will be formed.
Funds for permanent markers for the Town's oldest houses and major historical sites will be sought in anarti-
cle requested for insertion in the warrant for the 1975 Annual Town Meeting. In 1976, a budget will be prepared
and voters will be asked to approve funds for the celebration. All of the meetings of the 250th Anniversary Com-
mittee are open to those interested in participating, or who have ideas on what should be included.

Conservation Commission - Town Forest Committee
The Conservation Commission and Town Forest Committee have four major goals and responsibilities: 1. To
set aside at least 4,000 of the Town's nearly 20,000 acres for the purpose of protecting and preserving our Town's
character and natural resources; 2. To develop these areas with recreational, educational, and aesthetic programs,
including fishing and hunting in designated areas; 3. To conduct investigations and to hold public hearings related
to the Wetlands Protection Act; and 4. To help other groups in the community concerned with air pollution, water
pollution, roadside beautification, and other projects in natural areas. To these ends, the Conservation Commission
has, during 1974:
A. Asked chases. for, and received from the voters at Town Meeting, a sum of $50,000.00 to be used for land pur-
B. Through a self-help program approved by the Department of Natural Resources acquired a parcel of 30
acres off West Townsend Road formerly owned by Mrs. Dhu Johnson.
C. Accepted a gift of land from General Builders Supply, Inc. This wooded lot is located in the Rangeley
Road/West Street area.
D. Recommended that Lancaster Avenue, Mulpus Road, Northfield Road, Cross Street, Holman Street, and
Elmwood Road be designated as Scenic Roads.
E. Continued the Naturalist-At-Large Program, which each month guides Townspeople on Town-owned
and private properties and provides interpretive talks at each. In Maya record number of 75 persons hiked
into Woodruff Meadow, the first tour to be held on privately owned land.
F. Assisted elementary, middle, and high school teachers and students using Town-owned lands for field
trips and worked with the Ritter Memorial Library in preparing a display entitled "Tips for Outdoor Ex-
plorers".
G. Continued to serve as the regulatory agency of the Massachusetts Wetlands Protection Act, conducting
two public hearings. The Commission also issued six requests for compliance, conducted six site inspec-
tions, and issued one cease and desist order.
Other events which mark the year 1974 include: Acceptance of the Worcester County Conservation District
OUTSTANDING CONSERVATION COMMISSION Award for our programs in 1973; Investigation into wetlands map-
ping and zoning; Vandalism on Town property has continued to be a problem such as fires, broken bottles and cans
strewn over trails, destruction of signs and fences, which give sorry testimony to respect for the common lands. As
a safety measure, the old building at the Ben Normand area and the old Boy Scout cabin off Gilchrest Street have
been scheduled to be razed.
Mr. Jeffrey Dodge and Mrs. Connie Fleming have been appointed as Associate Members to assist US in re-
search, site visits, property supervision and clerical work. A fund has been established in the memory of Mr. Robert
Burton, Lunenburg resident and sportsman, by his friends and family.
The Conservation Commission and Town Forest Committee are struggling in a race with time. Open spaces
are less and less conspicuous in Lunenburg, squeezed between the tax bill and the developer's shovel. More con-
struction means fewer wildlife, fewer trails for hiking and skiing, fewer fields for hunting, and fewer woodland
streams for fishing.
To preserve the quality of our life and our "greenbelt" character, we must be able to keep up with the many
small feet of progress. Our woodlands, our wetlands, our waterways, our wildlife, and our people all need room
to breathe.

Dog Officer
With the adoption of an amendment to the Town
By-laws calling for confinement of female dogs during
their breeding seasons, a large decrease in the number
of dog packs was evidenced. The fact remains, however,
that in congested areas such as the Center and Whalom
where many homes house one, two or more dogs, it is not
unusual to see ten to fifteen dogs roaming about, but this
group is much less of a hazard to the public when female
dogs are not allowed to run at large during their breeding
seasons.
The problem of dogs running loose in the area of
the schools has been vastly improved due to the excellent
cooperation of school personnel enabling these animals
to be identified and owners contacted.
The bi-weekly check at the Municipal Refuse Dis-
posal Area has almost eliminated the nuisance of stray dogs
at this facility and has significantly reduced the number
of strays in the Hickory Hills Lake Area. Several incidents
of attacks upon livestock and fowl were investigated dur-
"Feeding dish of a starving animal"
ing 1974.
The picture to the left shows what is said to be an
indestructible feeding dish. The dish has over 100 teeth marks and numerous dents and tears inflicted by a starving
animal. The in question was taken into custody at the Municipal Refuse Disposal Area and after feeding the dog
your Dog Officer heard a loud disturbance and upon investigation found that the animal had thoroughly mangled the
feeding dish. It seems that the dog was so starved it attempted to devour the dish. This poor animal at one time most
likely had a good owner, but with no means of identification and after straying from home, took its own course in
survival and ran wild hunting for food. Don't let this happen to your pet.
This incident like many others could have been avoided if each dog wore a suitable collar and license tag and
were kept under close family supervision. This collar and license tag should be checked weekly by a family member
and should the license tag ever become lost, a replacement tag may be purchased in the Town Clerk's Office for a
mere .10c. This will help to avoid many unfortunate incidents of lost and starving pets.
Extension Service
Many of the young people of our community are involved in 4-H work including such projects as the Horse Club,
the Cooking Club and the Crafts Club. The 4-H leaders have devoted many hours of their time to helping young people
with a given project. This year some of the events the Clubs participated in were the Fourth of July Parade, the
Homecoming Parade and the Grange Fair, along with having two Horse Shows and a Parent Horse Show. Also held
were monthly and weekly meetings. An Awards Picnic for Club members and their parents was held in June at the
North Leominster Rod and Gun Club.
The Annual 4-H Cookie Sale was held in October and the top salesmen were Patty Cutting, Cindy Smith and
Robin DeFazio who received free camperships for their efforts. This summer, as in the past many Club members at-
tended summer which is open to all 4-H members. Also, much interest and participation went into Presentation
Night which was held on a County and State level. Congratulations to all who won ribbons.
-23-
===PAGE 26===
Veterans' Services
Upon reviewing the records on file in this Depart-
ment, there were 211 cases assisted financially for the per-
iod January 1, 1974 to December 31, 1974, at a cost of
approximately $3,283.17 monthly. We have tried to hold
the line as far as expenditures were concerned, but the
State did authorize a 4.2 cost of living increase to keep
up with the inflation and recession nationwide.
Labor disputes and unemployment were the main ele-
ments which caused Veterans to seek aid during the period
described, although sickness played an important role. I
would say that the lowing percenti represent the mag-
nitude of financial assistance in a department such as ours:
Labor Disputes 10%, Unemployment 45%, Si ckness 10%,
and Age 35%. The Veteran and his dependents would rather
be assisted by US than go to the State Welfare Department.
We have placed some Veterans in the employment
category through some organizations which we cooperate
with; we have assisted the Veterans and dependents in
filing questionnaires for the Veterans' Administration;
Veterans' Agent, Alfred H. Gauthier
and we have received pensions that were accepted by the
Veterans' Administration, making the Veteran or depen-
dent self-sustaining by getting their incomes into the plateau where they can survive, or exist, if you will. This
department is always on call to assist all Veterans requesting assistance with regard to the Veterans' Administration
or with the Social Security Department. I wish to thank all those who assisted in any way during 1974.
Inspector of Plumbing & Gas Fitting
During 1974 there were 124 Plumbing and Gas Per-
mits issued as follows: 75 Plumbing Permits for new and
remodeled dwellings, 5 Plumbing Permits for commercial
buildings, 43 Gas Permits for new and older dwellings,
and 1 Gas Permit for a commercial building.
Homeowners are reminded that when a domestic
water tank becomes defective and is replaced, the local
Inspector of Plumbing must inspect all new installations of
hot water tanks to ascertain that the heaters comply in all
respects to M.G. Chapter 142, Sections 17 and 19.
The above cited law relative to domestic hot water
tanks, tankless heaters and relief valves was enacted to
provide the citizens of our Commonwealth with the utmost
protection against boiler explosion and faulty manufactur-
ing in the sale and installation of these devices.
For further clarification of the Code, the Plumbing
Inspector is available to answer any questions.
Thomas E. Mortis

===PAGE 27===
Town Nurses
During 1974, a total of 1,364 Nursing Visits were made classified as follows: Bedside Nursing, 989; Adult
Health Promotion, 137; Visits to School Children, 141; Pre-School, 42; Infant & Premature Infants, 30; Pre-Natal
& Post Natal, 25. The Nursing fee is $9.50 per visit. However, no patient is refused service because of inability
to pay. "Well Oldsters" Clinics were started in the fall of 1974 at the Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly and at
the United Parish Building. These programs were held once a month and the response to the clinics has been very
gratifying to all concerned. It is planned to continue these programs in 1975.
Through the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, eight Well-Child Clinics wereheld with a pediatrician as
attending physician. A total of 62 infants and pre-schoolers made 80 visits for health supervision and physical exam-
inations. Also, immunizations were given as needed for Diphtheria, Tetanus and Pertussis Series & Boosters, Triva-
lent Oral Polio Series & Boosters, Measles, German Measles, Mumps, Small Pox Vaccinations, and Tuberculosis
Tine Testing. Information regarding Well-Child Clinics may be obtained by calling the Nurses' Office at 582-6702.
The Lunenburg Public Health Nursing Service is certified by the Federal Government to care for Medicare and
Medicaid patients. Also, the Board of Health (Nursing Service) has contracts with: Burbank Coordinated Home
Health Care for Nursing which enables patients referred from this program to have Bedside Nursing, Home Health
Aides, Physio, Occupational and Speech Therapy, and also Social Service, if needed; Homemaker Home Health
Aide Service of Montachusett for patients who need only skilled nursing and Home Health Aides; and the Nashoba
Associated Boards of Health for Social Workers.
The need for another part-time nurse is inevitable due to the increased school duties under the Chapter 766
Program and also the extensive Visiting Nurse caseload. The Nurses' have kept abreast of the time by attending
in-service meetings at Nashoba, Burbank and Leominster Hospitals, and Workshops given by the State. Effective
January 1, 1975, Mrs. Janet N. Pace will be the Supervising Nurse replacing Mrs. Doris M. Lahti who is retiring
after more than twenty years of dedicated service to the Town. The Nurses wish to thank the Board of Health, the
Nashoba Associated Boards of Health and the Advisory Committee for their continued help and guidance.
Town Nurses: left to right - Jean E. Hobbs, Shirley M. Carpenter and Supervising Nurse Janet N. Pace.

Highway Department
Opposition to Federal Fund Cut for Rural Roads!
Two-thirds of the nation's Federal Aid Secondary
Roads stand to lose further Federal funding after mid-1976
unless aroused citizens and organizations can persuade
their Congressmen that certain language in the Highway
Act must be amended. It is urged that the word "major"
be deleted from a subsection of the 1973 Federal funding
eligibility for secondary mileage. Unless this wording is
removed from the Highway Act, it would mean a reduc-
tion of two-thirds of the 600,000 miles of FAS highways
eligible for Federal funding.
No one from Federal Government has even sugges-
ted that the rural area roads would be the recipient of ad-
ditional monies from the FAS budget, although there is a
distinct possibility of a Federal tax hike on gasoline and
fuel oil. The rural road program would only suffer more
ifa Federal tax is imposed due to loss in revenue from de-
creased gasoline sales.
(Excerpt from editorial by Van Roekel, Director for Na-
George G. Munyon, Jr., Highway Superintendent
tional Association of Counties)
The following is an explanation of services performed and projects accomplished by the Highway Department
on the Town's 76.97 miles of accepted roads:
Chapter 81 - 721.60 tons of Bituminous Concrete complete in place was applied to Oak Avenue. John Street was
reconstructed. 131.25 tons of patch was used on roads all over Town. Brush was cut on Lancaster, South Row,
Shirleyand Flat Hill Roads. Guard rail was installed on various roads. 1189 yards of screened sand was purchased
for use where required. Spring cleaning and drainage work was also done under this account. The total spent from
January 1 to June 30, 1974 was $26,937.92, added to the calendar year of 1973 of $26,937.86 makes a total of
$53,875.78 spent for the 18 month transitional period ending June 30, 1974.
Chapter 90 Improvement - 24,729 gallons of asphalt was applied to Page, Arbor, South Row, Pond, Kimball, Hol-
man, Northfield, Hollis, Whiting, John, Laurel Lane, Maple Parkway, Crest, Cushing and Charlton Streets. 1,734
yards of sand was used for cover. 205.60 tons of patch was used on various roads. Guard rail was installed on El-
ectric, Chase and Lancaster Avenue. Drainage was installed in Arbor, Natick, John, White, Reservoir, Shirley-
Leominster, Lancaster, Page, Howard and Electric Avenue. 552.85 tons of Bituminous Concrete laid complete in
place was applied to Lancaster Avenue. Catch basin cleaning, spring cleaning and sweeping were also accomplished
under this account at a cost of $42,733.93.
General Highway Maintenance - 101.70 tons of patch were used to repair holes in various roads. 35 gallons of
weed killer was used to kill poison ivy and brush along the roadsides. South Row Road was reconstructed. Minor
drainage problems were corrected, guard rail installed, brush cut, signs and small tools purchased. The total spent
from January 1 to June 30, 1974 was $6,954.82, added to the calendar year of 1973 of $23,225.09 makes a total
of $30,179.91 spent for the 18 month transitional period ending June 30, 1974.

loaming was done under this account. Guard rail was installed. Continuation ofreconstruction of South Row Road
was accomplished. Calcium chloride, small tools and construction signs were purchased. The total spent for the
last six months of 1974 was $17,309.17.

Police Department
The yet unsolved homicide of a Fitchburg girl whose
body was found in Lunenburg and five persons losing their
CHIEF.
lives on Route 2A has made 1974 another tragedy-ridden
year for this community.
Despite increasing concentration on stricter enforce-
ment of motor vehicle laws, the traffic accident toll in
1974 continued to climb. The number of motor vehicle
complaints in 1974 was 1098, which is substantially the
same as in 1973, but four times higher than the 267 com-
plaints issued in 1972.
After the purchase of a breathalyzer in 1974, 96
persons were taken into custody for driving under the in-
fluence. Of these, 59 had readings above the minimum
allowed under law and 31 persons refused to take the test.
The conviction record to date of these 90 persons is 62
guilty, 6 not guilty and 22 cases still pending. The re-
maining six were released as their readings were below the
minimum.
Frederick B. Hobbs, Chief of Police
The breathalyzer was purchased for $995.00 which
was completely reimbursed to the Town by the Governor's
Highway Safety Bureau. Having our own equipment and trained men resulted in a savings of $25.00 per test admin-
istered, based on charges for using facilities of the Fitchburg Police Department, as was necessary in the past.
the list of needs is an efficient records keeping system. It is recommended that this be accomplished through
the services of the Massachusetts Police Institute, affiliated with the Massachusetts Police Chiefs' Association. Free
institute staffassistance is available and one halfof the cost of supplies and equipment required to implement the new
system is also provided.

Fire Department
In 1974 the Fire Department answered 327 calls for
assistance as follows: Rescue Calls 123; Service Calls 64;
Brush or Grass Fires 48; Car Fires 23; Mutual Aid 15; Ac-
cidents 9; Buildings 12; Cooking Fires 6; Chimney Fires 6;
Needless 7; Town Dump 4; Stove 3; False Alarms 2; Rail-
road Car 1; Bomb Threat 1; T.V. 1; Dryer 1; Electrical 1.
This is an increase of 34 calls over last year. Rescue Unit
calls were down by 19, Brush and Grass Fires were up by
19. Service calls were up by 27 calls. Other categories
showed slight changes.
Our Rescue Unit and Fire Department services have
received many compliments throughout the year for their
efficient operation and dedication of the men who perform
a 24 hour service.
Seven of our Rescue personnel who have completed
Emergency Medical Technician (EMT) Course are regis-
tered with the National Registry and the State Department
of Public Health. Another six are expected to become
EMT's in 1975. State Law requires that all Rescue per-
James P. Deming, Fire Chief
sonnel be EMT's by July 1976.
-29-
===PAGE 32===
Our building needs are becoming more critical. With a truck replacement planned, there will not be room
to walk between the trucks. There are no provisions for drying of hose and no space available for the equipment nec-
essary to up-date our Fire Alarm. As the alarm is set up now, we have no way to notify our men for emergency when
we lose our electrical power. Our siren should be replaced with air horns, air tanks and compressor, and a gener-
ator installed.
The Fire truck we are asking to have replaced is a 1952 chassis with a high pressure pump that is about 35
years old. A fire truck has a life expectancy of about twenty years.
The Fire Department meetings and drills were held on a bi-monthly basis to increase our knowledge and im-
prove our technique and firefighting skills.
I would like to take this opportunity to thank the officers and members of the Fire Department for their help
and cooperation in making this organization one of which the Town can be most proud.

Inspector of Weights & Measures
The user of any commercial weighing or measuring
device is now legally responsible for its accuracy. He is
compelled by law to cause such device to be inspected,
tested and serviced as often as necessary to insure that such
device is correct. In addition, the Inspector of Weights
and Measures, who is appointed each year by the Board
of Selectmen, may inspect and test any weighing or mea-
suring device as often as he deems necessary.
In 1974, 31 scales and 40 weights were inspected.
Of this number, 2 scales were condemned. Forty-three
gasoline pumps were inspected and 1 pumpwas condemned.
In accordance with the provisions of M.G.L.A.
Chapter 98, Section 41, all inhabitants or persons having
usual places of business within the Town using weighing
or measuring devices for the purpose of buying or selling
-
0
goods, wares or merchandise, for public weighing or for
18
16
hire or reward, are hereby notified to bring them in to be
11
retested, adjusted and sealed.
8
ID
The law also provides that any person who neglects
Wayne A. Balcom
or refuses to exhibit his weighing or measuring devices to
a Sealer or who hinders or in any way interferes with a
Sealer in the performance of his duty, shall be punished
by a fine of not more than fifty dollars.
Development & Industrial Commission
Another year has passed with limited activity by the Development and Industrial Commission--based primarily
upon the fact that whatever inquiries were madeby either industry or developers resulted in no further discussion once
the matter of an adequate water supply was discussed. The plans this Commission formulated for limited industrial
growth in small parks away from the population centers as a means to broaden our tax base may never be realized so
long as water remains a problem.
We all know what a difficult time the Water District has had regarding expansion. The District hasbeen turned
down repeatedly in its attempt to improve service for its present users. As each new user is added, and they must be
according to law, service will eventually suffer. As each day passes, the cost to make any improvements is accel-
erating. The Development and Industrial Commission feels this area needs special attention now. We can no longer
afford to be like an ostrich with its head in the sand. It is not so unrealistic to consider facing a problem of water
rationing in the future, especially during a dry period. The drought of a few years ago cannot be overlooked, es-
pecially when there are developers approaching the Town now with the intent to build more new homes and many of
these would be located within the Water District.
For these reasons the Development and Industrial Commission will propose at Town Meeting a Water Study Com-
mittee be immediately set up to review the Town's water supply so that there is an overall plan developed for the
future. This plan, complete with timetables and estimated costs where possible, must clearly define the best alter-
natives open to the Town and/or Water District, so that the District's users and the Town's taxpayers are afforded
maximum protection and service.

IN MEMORY OF
Rosaire Chapdelaine was not only very well
known in the area as a farmer and business-
man, but also as an individual who possessed
a keen awareness and respect for his fellow-
man. Undoubtedly "Rosaire" will best be
remembered by many for his attendance and
active participation in the deliberations at
Town Meetings.
ROSAIRE CHAPDELAINE
1905 - 1974
IN MEMORY OF
William A. Mason, Jr. served as Chairman and
member of the Lunenburg Planning Board for ten
years and was the first Chairman of the Monta-
chusett Regional Planning Commission. He was
a graduate of Bentley School of Accounting, a
Navy Veteran of World War II and a member
of many civic and professional organizations in
the area. Prior to retirement "Bill" was the
proprietor of a Mobile Home Park in Lunenburg.
WILLIAM A. MASON, JR.
1912 - 1974

Excerpts From Special Town Meeting Minutes
January 28, 1974
Article 1. To see if the Town will vote to appropriate from the Surplus Revenue Account the sum of $5,772.00 the Town's
share of highway work to be performed in accordance with the provisions of Chapter 81 of the General Laws, or act anything
thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 2. To see if the Town will vote to appropriate from the Surplus Revenue Account the sum of $21, 166.00 to pay the
State share of Chapter 81 highway work in anticipation of an allotment from the State, this sum to be restored to the Surplus Reve-
nue Account upon receipt of said allotment from the State, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 3. To see if the Town will vote to direct the Selectmen to petition the Commissioner of Public Works for the Com-
monwealth of Massachusetts, requesting that the proposed relocation of Route 2A in Lunenburg be abandoned, and that new plans
be prepared limiting the work on said highway to correcting the dangerous curves without the necessity of taking of homes or ex-
tensive land takings and, further, to petition the Legislature to pass a bill to the same effect, or to take any action thereon.
VOTED.
Article 4. To see if the Town will vote to accept the amendment to Chapter 40 of the General Laws which inserts after
Section 15B the following section: Section 15C, Upon recommendation or request of the planning board, conservation commission,
or the historical commission of any city or town, such city or town may designate any road in such city or town other than a num-
bered route or state highway as a scenic road.; or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO PASS OVER ARTICLE 4.
Article 5. To see if the Town will vote to designate Leominster Road in its entirety as a scenic road, under Section 15C,
Chapter 40 MGLA, or act anything thereon. VOTED.

Article 2. To accept the regular annual written reports of the Town Officers and Committees, and to hear the recommen-
dations MOUSLY. of the Building Committee per Article 26 of the 1973 Annual Town Meeting, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANI-
Article 3. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XV of the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of
the Town of Lunenburg "by deleting the Classification and Salary Schedule voted on March 4, 1973 to be effective March, 1974,
and substituting therefor the Classification and Salary Schedule proposed by the Personnel Committee and appearing on Page 15 of
the 1973 Town Report, such action to be effective July 1, 1974, or act anything thereon. (Approved by the Finance Committee.)
VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 4. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XV of the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of
Town of Lunenburg" by (a) deleting from Section 11 "New Personnel" the words "and their hiring rates" at the end of paragraph
(a); by (b) adding at the end of Section 17 "Paid Vacations" the following: "After completion of 20 years continuous service 25
days (5 calendar weeks). "; by (c) adding after the second paragraph of Section 19 "Sick Leave" the following new paragraph:
"Two personal days may be taken in any one twelve month period. These days are to be deducted from accumulated sick leave.
Personal days shall be taken with the approval of the Department Head. "; and by (d) deleting from Section 23 "Longevity Pay"
the words ",and for any part-time employee the amount shall be reduced pro-rata to the length of his average work week during
the most recent 24 months of service. ", and substituting therefor the following words: "or 1% of his current annual salary rate,
whichever is greater. For any part-time employee the amount shall be reduced pro-rata to a portion of $5.00 per year based on
the length of his average work week during the most recent 24 months of service, or to 1% of his current annual salary rate(based
on the length of his average work week during the most recent 24 months of service), whichever is greater.", or act anything
thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 5. Shall the Town, inaddition to the payment of fifty per cent of a premium for contributory group life and health
insurance for employees in the service of the Town and their dependents, pay a subsidiary or additional rate? (Approved by the
Finance Committee to be effective January 1, 1975). VOTED.

of money necessary to defray the charges and expenses of the Town for the ensuing year and to fix for the fiscal year 1974-1975
the salaries and compensation of all elected officers of the Town andany other items included in the budget of the Finance Com-
mittee, or act anything thereon. (Budget approved as recommended.) VOTED TO RAISE BY TAXATION AND APPROPRIATE

the westerly side of Chase Road: Beginning at the most southeasterly corner thereof on the westerly side of Chase Road at a point
measuring southerly 50 feet from land of Seliina A. Heikkila and at the present boundary between Outlying and commercial;

Article 14. To see if the Town will accept an amendment to Section IV of the Montachusett Regional Vocational Techni-
cal School District Agreement which was proposed by the Regional School District Committee on January 16, 1974, which de-
letes Section F - Special Operating Costs under Article IV, and inserts: "F. Special Operating Costs i. The phrase "special
operating costs used herein shall be interpreted to mean all expenses and costs, whether direct or indirect, incurred in opera-
tion of any program other than the regular Regional Vocational Technical School program and shall include, but not be limited to,
costs of heating, lighting, maintenance, transportation, instruction, supervision, supplies, salaries, wages. ii. The Committee
shall determine the operating costs for each calendar year of any evening trade extension courses or other types of courses or pro-
grams which are offered by the District to persons other than pupils attending the regular day Regional Vocational School. Each
member municipality's share of such operating costs shall be determined by computing the ratio which that municipality's enroll-
ment of persons in such courses or programs during that calendar year for which the apportionment is determined bears to the total
enrollment of persons in such courses or programs. III. The assessment of such special operating costs as so determined shall be
separately set forth in each budget so as to be identifiable as such. ", or act anything thereon. VOTED.
Article 15. To see if the Town will vote to amend its By-laws by adding under Article V the following new Section to be
known as Section 12: "No person shall drink any alcoholic beverages as defined in Chapter 138, Section 1 of the General Laws
while in, on, or upon any public way, or upon any way to which the public has a right of access, or in any place to which mem-
bers of the public have access as invitees or licensees, park or playground, or private land or place, without consent of the owner
or person in control thereof. All alcoholic beverages beingused in violation of this By-law shall be seized and safely held until
final adjudication of the charge against the person or persons summoned before the court, at which time they shall be returned to
the person entitled to lawful possession. Whoever violatesany portion of this Section shall be punished by a fine of not more than
fifty dollars ($50.00) for each offense." or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.

QUESTION NO. 1 - PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General Court
in a joint session of the two branches held August 25, 1971, received 212 votes in the affirmative and 39 in the negative, and in
a joint session of the two branches held June 6, 1973, received 235 votes in the affirmative and 19 in the negative?
SUMMARY - The proposed constitutional amendment, if approved, would replace the present Article 52 of the Articles of
Amendment to the Constitution of the Commonwealth and would empower the General Court, by concurrent vote of the two houses,
to take a recess or recesses amounting to not more than thirty days. The present Article 52 permits such recesses but provides that
"no such recess shall extend beyond the sixtieth day" from the beginning of the legislative session.
VOTE:
YES - 1597
NO - 649
BLANKS 546
QUESTION NO. 2 - PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General Court
in a joint session of the two branches held August 25, 1971, received 177 votes in the affirmative and 65 in the negative, and in
a joint session of the two branches held June 6, 1973, received 166 votes in the affirmative and 93 in the negative?
SUMMARY - The proposed constitutional amendment, if approved, would provide for a census in the year 1975 and every
tenth year thereafter of the inhabitants of each city and town as a basis for determining the representative, senatorial and councillor
districts for the ten year period beginning with the first Wednesday in the fourth January following the taking of the census, pro-
-84-
===PAGE 87===
vided that the districts as established based on the 1971 census, shall terminate on the first Wednesday in January, 1979. The
census shall specify the number of inhabitants residing in each precinct of a town and each precinct and ward of a city.
Under the proposed amendment, the House of Representatives would consist of 160 members, in contrast to the present member-
ship of 240, and the Senate of 40 members. The General Court would, at its first regular session after the year in which the census
is taken, divide the Commonwealth into 160 representative districts and 40 senatorial districts of contiguous territory so that each
representative and each senatorwill represent an equal number of inhabitants as nearly as may be; and such districts shall be formed,
as nearly as may be, without-uniting two counties or parts of two or more counties and, with respect to representative districts,
without uniting, as nearly as may be two towns or parts of two or more towns, two cities or parts of two or more cities, or a city
and a town, or parts of cities and towns, into one district, and without dividing any town containing less than 2500 inhabitants.
The General Court would be permitted to pass laws to limit the time within which judicial proceedings may be instituted calling
in question any such division. The proposed amendment further provides that every representative, for one year at least immedi-
ately preceding his election, shall have been an inhabitant of the district for which he is chosen, and every senator shall be an
inhabitant of this Commonwealth for five years at least preceding election and at the time of his election shall be an inhabitant
of the district for which he is chosen. Every representative and senator shall cease to represent his district when he shall cease
to be an inhabitant of the Commonwealth. The manner of calling and conducting the elections for representatives and for senators
and councillors, and of ascertaining their election, shall be prescribed by law. The amendment vests original jurisdiction in the
Supreme Judicial Court, upon petition of any voter of the Commonwealth, filed with the clerk of said court, for judicial relief
relative to the establishment of House of Representatives, councillors and senatorial districts.
VOTE:
YES 1958
NO 314
BLANKS 520
QUESTION NO. 3 - PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General Court
in a joint session of the two branches held June 7, 1972, received 250 votes in the affirmative and 3 in the negative, and in a
joint session of the two branches held June 6, 1973, received 253 votes in the affirmative and 0 in the negative?
SUMMARY The proposed constitutional amendment would repeal Section 2 of Article 46 of the Articles of Amendments to
the Constitution of the Commonwealth (the "anti-aid amendment", so-called), and replace it with a new Section 2, for the purpose
of allowing grants in aid to private higher educational institutionsor to students, or parents or guardians of students, attending such
institutions.
The proposed amendment would delete the first clause of the present Section 2, which requires that all moneys raised by
taxation in the towns and cities for the support of public schools, and all moneys appropriated by the Commonwealth for the support
of common schools shall be applied to or expended in only those schools conducted according to law under the order and super-
intendence of the authorities of the town or city in which the money is expended. The effect of the deletion of the first clause of
Section 2 would be to remove the constitutional prohibition against the use of public moneys, which have been raised by taxation
or appropriated for support of public schools, for grants in aid to private higher educational institutions or to students, or parents
or guardians of students, attending such institutions.
The proposed amendment would also alter the second clause of the present Section 2 by removing the prohibition against a
grant, appropriation or use of public money or property or loan of public credit by the Commonwealth or any political subdivision
for the purpose of founding, maintaining or aiding any school or institution of learning, whether under public control or otherwise,
wherein any denominational doctrine is inculcated, or any other school or any college which is not publicly owned and under the
exclusive control, order and superintendence of public officers or public agents. In place of the foregoing, the proposed amend-
ment would prohibit the grant, appropriation or use of public money or property or loan of credit by the Commonwealth or any
political subdivision for the purpose of founding, maintaining or aiding any primary or secondary school which is not publicly owned
and under the exclusive control, order and superintendence of public officers or public agents.
The proposed amendment would also add to the present Section 2a provision that nothing therein shall be construed to pro-
hibit grants in aid to private higher educational institutions or to students, or parents, or guardians of students, attendingsuch
institutions.
VOTE:
YES - 1227
NO - 1017
BLANKS - 548
QUESTION NO. 4 LAW PROPOSED BY AN INITIATIVE PETITION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General Court
in a joint session of the two branches held May 17, 1972, received 131 votes in the affirmative and 121 in the negative, and in
a joint session of the two branches held May 8, 1974, received 139 votes in the affirmative and 119 in the negative?
SUMMARY The proposed constitutional amendment would revise Article 78 of the Articles of Amendment to the Constitution
to permit the expenditure of money from the highway fund for mass transportation lines and other mass transportation purposes in
such manner as the Legislature may direct. The highway fund includes receipts from duties, excises and license taxes relating
to registration, operation or use of motor vehicles and taxes from the sale of motor vehicle fuels. The expenditure of money from
such fund is presently restricted to highway and bridge construction, reconstruction, maintenance and repair, enforcement of state
traffic laws, and administration of the tax statutes which provide highway fund receipts.
VOTE:
YES 1077
NO - 1170
BLANKS 545
-85-
===PAGE 88===
QUESTION NO. 5 - LAW PROPOSED BY AN INITIATIVE PETITION
Do you approve of a law summarized below which was disapproved in the House of Representatives by a vote of 73 in the affirmative
and 150 in the negative and was disapproved in the Senate by a vote of 16 in the affirmative and 22 in the negative?
SUMMARY - Provisions of the act establish an independent Corrupt Practices Commission, with five members to be appointed
by the Governor to staggered five year terms. The Commissionh subpoena powers and is to investigate, by means of secret hear-
ings, candidates' compliance with all state and federal laws relating to political campaign contributions and expenditures and
corrupt practices. If probable cause is shown, the Commission is required to direct the Attorney General to take further action in
the proper form and is required to make public a report of such action. In cases involving the campaign practices of the Attorney
General himself, a special attorney is to be appointed. If no probable cause is shown, the Commission is to state so publicly.
Further, the proposed act extends the application of the current statute regarding the disclosure of campaign expenditures
and contributions, to all candidates for office above the town and city level, with the exception of President and Vice President
of the United States. The act requires all such candidates to receive and disburse all amounts greater than $25 by check, and makes
it a crime to knowingly receive cash payment from a candidate or his committee fora service costing more than $25. The actalso
requires candidates to designate a singlebank as depository of funds and as recordkeeper, with records of receipts and expenditures
to be open to public scrutiny.
Provisions of the act extend present campaign spending limits to cover all media expenses and require all media firms (in-
cluding television, radio, newspaper, billboard, magazine, advertising, public relations, printing, opinion polling, computer,
telephone, telegraph) to report the purchase of media services by candidates. Media expenses of candidates for the offices of
district attorney, clerk of court, register of probate and insolvency, register of deeds, county commissioner, county treasurer and
sheriff are limited to $.07 for each resident of the respective electorial district. Other provisions of the act require candidates,
upon official announcement or filing of nomination papers, to report all political receipts and expenditures since the date of the
last general election for the office sought, and count such expenditures toward spending limits. The act also limits candidates to
one political committee.
The act establishes a maximum penalty of one year imprisonment and/or $10,000 fine for individuals engaged in corrupt
practices, and a $50,000 fine for corporations so involved. In addition, the Attorney General is permitted to bring an action for
the removal of a candidate whose election was materially aided by corrupt practices on the part of the candidate or one acting in
his behalf, where such removal is otherwise authorized by law.
VOTE:
YES - 1448
NO - 758
BLANKS - 586

Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District
This year, our day school enrollment of 1,152 representedar increase over 1973 of 56 students. The potential
enrollment for next year is 1, 210. During 1974, 1,295 adults were prepared for gainful employment through our
Evening Occupational Programs an increase of 257 over 1973.
A six-week Summer Program and the Afternoon Programs reached 497 additional students not enrolled at Mon-
tachusett Regional. These students come from your communities for Job Entry Skills and Career Exploration Programs.
To maximize the full use of the facilities, priorities have been established for these programs to prepare students not
enrolled in our school particularly the Seniors and the "just graduated" for Job Entry Skills.
Special emphasis is placed on Adult Programming -- evenings and summers -- for unemployed and underem-
ployed. Local industry representatives have made requests and provided job opportunities as a result of the short-
unit upgrading skills in many of our Adult Vocational Programs. As in the past, we have been provided with tech-
nical input, through our Craft Advisory Committees, to techniques in changing skills updated in all our courses.
Local industry representatives and local service agencies have contributed time, materials and, in numerous cases,
Co-op Work Study stations to our students, with resultant changes in course content. This method asbecome stan-
dard operating procedure; hence, in preparation for accreditation through a Self-Evaluation System, courses have
been updated and published.
Though we have not reachedour full growth cycle, Montachusett Regional has provided both Job Entry Skills
and Career Education to an even greater number than was envisioned when the school was designed for 1, 000students.
The School Committee, Administration and Advisory Committees worked together in developing proposals un-
der Chapter 766 to provide for students with Special Needs: physically, mentally, or socially handicapped. Addi-
tional staff and programs have been developed, including a School Psychologist, Learning Disabilities Specialist and
a Special Needs Supervisor for student adjustment programs. Mr. David P. Atkins, new Coordinator of Pupil Per-
sonnel Services (replacing Mr. Orren F. Bradstreet) contributed to the reorganization of the department and produced
greater involvement for students with Special Needs. Core Evaluation Teams have been established and are func-
tioning, resulting in special programs for individuals on a continuing basis.
Mr. John 1. White has been a catalyst in Career Education throughout the region and has promoted additional
liaison between the sending schools and Montachusett Regional. He is also the liaison in "Project Redesign" which
is developing a model for more creative Career Education for students in the "general" education category through-
out the region.
The Staff have labored very diligently developing and formalizing a philosophy and have made recommenda-
tions for improving teaching methodology and facilities. Accreditation evaluation will take place in March 1975.
The School Committee developed a policy and has, therefore, initiated a formal House Construction Program.
Many projects were completed for the various cities and towns in the District, amounting to $12,092.52. In addi-
tion, $12,824.00 worth of printing was provided, at an approximate saving to your communities of $8,256.00. Our
Data Processing Department provided such services as: Contract Status Reports and Carpooling System - Montachu-
sett Regional Planning Commission; Student Scheduling, Financial Accounting Reporting System and Payroll Projec-
tions - Lunenburg; Student Scheduling and Attendance Accounting - Winchendon; a questionnaire for the League of
Women Voters - Harvard; Crime and Incident Analysis Reports - Fitchburg Police Department; Municipal Accounting
System - Sterling; and Financial Accounting Reporting System - Quabbin Regional, Barre.
Of 118 graduates, 16.2% have gone on to Technical Institutes, Community Colleges, etc., in pursuit of ad-
ditional education. Students who have completed post-graduate Health Programs at Montachusett Regional are con-
tinuing their education with advance standing in the quest of adegree. Three separate Graduation Ceremonies were
held to accommodate families and friends of the High School graduates, Allied Health Careers and the Post-Grad-
uate Programs.
-87-
===PAGE 90===
Our Annual Open House was held on February 12, 1974, with over 4,500 attending from all the District com-
munities. Also, many periodic tours were held for students and their parents during the year. This year, Open
House will again be held during American Vocational Week in February.
Student Activities include the Annual "Welcome Freshman Day", which is in the form of a student Carnival
and introduces the new students to our Club and extracurricular activities, the social environment of our school and
our Athletic Programs. Special Club activities included: Three Horse Shows, Two Drama presentations "Black
Comedy" "White Lies", and "Ten Little Indians"; the yearbook the "Odyssey", the school newspaper - "The
Growl", the Math Club paper "Poly Math". Fundshave been raised for student scholarships and for "The Annual
Carl A. Douglas Memorial Scholarship", in memory of a departed staff member. All are evidence of student iden-
tity and enthusiasm toward the school.
The Student Council and Student Advisory Council have made recommendations to the Administration and the
School Committee. These recommendations have been discussed, accepted, and implemented in areas of student
discipline, government, tenets of Chapter 622 and the Cafeteria. Through a Student/Faculty Discipline Board, they
have worked to develop schoolwide programs for improved citizenship. The School Committee has commended the
students' efforts in promoting positive social and cultural programs.
Requests have been made by several communities for expansion consideration to include new communities in
the District, as well as to increase the school capacity. A subcommittee of the School Committee is presently con-
sidering expansion proposals, particularly in view of the ever-increasing waiting lists. An expansion program would
emphasize additional career opportunities, rather than enlarge our present shops.
Federal monies received from project proposals to date amount to $668,358.00, of which $101,200.00 was
received this year. In addition, we have received the 50% reimbursement on operating cost provided by Chapter
74, Transportation and Interest, and S.B.A.B. Hence, it was possible to reduce the assessment to the cities and
towns by $2, 017, (52.87%). In spite of the spiralling economy and uncontrolled increases in many services, the
administration-in preparing its budget--is mindful of a worsening economy and is making every effort to reduce as-
sessments to the cities and towns without impairing the goals of the school.
At the annual reorganization, Mr. Kenneth H. Wilson, Jr., of Fitchburg was unanimously elected as Chair-
man of the School Committee. Mr. Herbert N. Hoffman is the elected representative from the Town of Lunenburg.
