No-School Signals - 1974-75
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after conferring with
the Chairman of the School Committee and consulting with the Highway Superintendent and the Police
to determine the condition of the roads.
The practice is to close schools only in case of extreme weather conditions or unforeseen
emergency; therefore, when schools are in session on stormy days, parents are urged to exercise
their personal judgment as to the wisdom of sending their children to school.
The following schedule of No-School Signals will be in effect for the current school year:
TWO SETS of two (2) blasts on the horn at the Central Fire
Station indicates no-school at the following times for the
groups indicated -
6:30 A. M.
Grades 8 - 12
7:15 A. M.
Grades K - 7
10:00 A. M.
P. M. Session - Kindergarten
Announcements will be carried on the regular broadcasts of Radio Stations WEIM and WFGL,
Fitchburg; WLMS, Leominster; as well as WTAG in Worcester, as promptly as the decision is made.
The cancelling of school sessions requires the completion of numerous separate telephone calls
as rapidly as possible. The crowding of telephone circuits can mean that the signal center or bus
contractors might not be notified on time. Parents can help by not allowing their children to make
unnecessary calls.

Annual Report of
Superintendent of Schools
I am pleased to submit my Fifth Annual Report as
Superintendent of Lunenburg Public Schools. This is
the 105th such report preserved in the Town Records
and covers the year ending December 31, 1974.
AN EDITORIAL FROM THE SUPERINTENDENT
OF SCHOOLS -
Last April, Lunenburg voters approved an opera-
ting school budget request of $2,573,377. This sum
WILLIAM R. HOLLAND
reflected a 12.4% increase over the previous year's
Superintendent of Schools
budget appropriation; a percentage increase which
also comes close to representing the average annual
school budget increase experienced in Lunenburg over the past five years.
As usual, over 80% of the budget increase was contributed to teacher salaries with the remain-
ing increases found in the hard-hit energy-related areas, particularly fuel oil and utilities.
Lunenburg, however, was fortunate last year when compared to budget increases in surrounding towns
since teachers had signed a two-year salary contract calling for an approximate 4% increase in
1974-75 and bus contractors were completing the third year of their bus contracts. These facts,
plus an unanticipated higher level of reimbursement for education and other sources from the State,
helped contribute to an approximate $8. decrease in the tax rate.
Unfortunately, keeping school costs down in FY-76 is an extremely difficult if not nearly im-
possible task. Increases in supplies, fuel oil and utilities continue to climb the inflationary
ladder. The awarding of new bus contracts and renegotiating of all employees contracts push costs
upward. New State laws like Chapter 766 (Special Education) and Chapter 622 (Equal Educational
Opportunity) also contribute to budget increases. These factors cause school officials to freeze
or cut present spending levels, eliminate any requests for needed new personnel, and abolish plans
for new programs or the major remodeling of buildings.
Although discouraging, these "belt tightening" measures are being taken in Lunenburg just like
they are being taken in other school districts throughout the country. In fact, in response to
rising unemployment and a suffering economy, local and state newspapers are carrying almost daily
summaries of school and municipal budgets that are being slashed. However, in spite of much
rhetoric in the press and many bold and genuine attempts to keep school costs down, factors beyond
the direct control of school, town, state, and yes, federal officials, continue to push costs
higher each year. With the annual cost-of-living spiraling upward in the vicinity of 12%, local
officials find themselves caught up in a real struggle to keep operating budgets at or below a 10
to 15% increase.
Meanwhile, because nearly 80% of the school budget and the school budget increase is in staff
salaries, school committee members in Lunenburg and elsewhere are spending an inordinate amount of
time in collective bargaining activities, particularly with teachers.
Lamenting the marked decline of candidates for school committee positions throughout the
State, a recent Worcester Evening Gazette editorial made this point:
"The job of school committee members has changed substantially in recent years. As
teachers have become organized in labor unions, school committee members everywhere have
found themselves less involved with educating children and more tied up with bargaining
about teachers rights and salaries.
"On the salary issue, school committees have found themselves largely in no-win
situations caught between a high pressure union and the paying public, damned if they
did and damned if they didn't.
-93-
===PAGE 96===
Although rather blunt, this editorial does define the problem. On one hand, teachers and
other staff employees are anxious to realize salary adjustments in order to meet the very real in-
creases in their cost-of-living. On the other hand, taxpayers are resisting ever-rising increases
in local taxes because they too are suffering from rising costs as well as suddenly finding them-
selves with less financial security. Add to this problem the increasing demands being placed upon
your local school system to provide more and improved programs and services to its children while
at the same time keeping its budget in line, it is no wonder there is a dearth of candidates for
school committee positions.
Quite simply, since our enrollment in Lunenburg has and will continue to remain quite stable
at around 2200 pupils, the only real way to significantly hold down school costs is to sacrifice
some of the current quality of our present educational system. For example, we would definitely
have to limit the objectives of the public school to teaching the basic skills. In order to
eliminate staff, class sizes would have to be increased from our current average of 25 pupils to
35 pupils, all extra-curricular programs, particularly the athletic and music programs, would have
to be eliminated, professional staff important to but not directly involved with classroom instruc-
tion (administration, guidance, library, etc.) would have to be significantly reduced, high school
elective courses would have to be eliminated or drastically reduced, and current attempts to pro-
vide students with occupational training and exposure to the fine, performing, and domestic arts
would have to be significantly curtailed.
Even these actions may be difficult to accomplish, since by statute, school systems are re-
quired to provide students with certain required educational experiences. Non-compliance with
certain State laws could mean loss of state funds or civil court action brought against the school
system by parents. State laws and Board of Education regulations clearly define minimum curriculum
standards as well as a host of other required programs and services for youngsters. Some laws and
regulations are many times SO specific that even the number of required instructional hours, the
number and type of required instructional staff, and the number and type of student to be served
is delineated. To make this situation even more alarming in these times of economic stress, is
the tendency on the State level each year to pass more laws that mandate expensive programs with-
out giving any guarantee of State funding to help ease the financial impact of these new programs
locally. Clearly, the financial burden of these new State mandates rests on the sagging shoulders
of the local community.
Further, since 1965, most school systems
in the Commonwealth, if not all of them, have
negotiated comprehensive contracts with
teachers and other employee groups within the
school system. Bold actions to reduce staff,
eliminate programs, increase class sizes, how-
ever necessary or feasible these actions might
be, come in direct conflict with some legally
binding provisions found in many negotiated
contracts.
What does all this mean? I think it
means that similar to the private sector, the
public school system is not immune to the
fall-out created by times of great economic
stress. Schools are currently directly
affected by vibrations which collectively
spell out the phrase, "hold the line on your
budget". If the economic situation worsens or
doesn't improve in the next year or so, it
means that similar to the private sector,
bolder attempts at reducing costs may have to be
taken by our school system and others through-
out Massachusetts. Such attempts or sacrifices,
however, have to be balanced against the
desires of the community. Specifically, what
worth does Lunenburg place on the quality of
The CLASS OF 1977 Homecoming 1974
its public school system? What expectations
does the community have for its graduates?
Float Entry ("Westford Ho; Lunen-
These are the essential questions when there
burg Go!") received "Honorable
Mention.
is only so much money to go around.
-94-
===PAGE 97===
Further, any attempts to keep school costs down cannot solely be a local activity but has to
be accompanied by leadership at the State level which must (1) declare a moratorium in mandating
new expensive programs; (2) reform the current school financing system (there are several tax
reform proposals pending, one of which calls for the State to assume a higher percentage of finan-
cial support for education locally) which puts an over-reliance on the local property tax, and (3)
concentrate on reducing the ever-increasing State Budget deficit which should enable the State to
meet its current financial obligations to local communities and hopefully give a greater priority
to educational funding in the future.
Finally, the public has to better understand the realities and constraints relating to reducing
school costs and cutting school budgets. This editorial has touched upon a few. Naturally, small
economies can be realized when you add up a series of budget cuts that have been made, for example
in a school system's teacher conference account, student field trip account, staff travel accounts,
and by eliminating two or three athletic programs (e. g. track and hockey). No one would deny
this. However, with salaries, transportation, fuel and utilities representing 90% of our school
budget total, the mathematical reality is that even with the complete elimination of our hockey and
indoor track programs, our travel and conference accounts, and student field trips, such a budget
cut would only represent a .001% reduction in Lunenburg's FY-76 School Operating Budget Request.
Such budget cuts generate much discussion and attention in the press and create numerous anxieties,
however, they fall way short of resolving the budget problem faced by Superintendents, School
Committees and Taxpayers each year.

- BILLBOARD WELCOMES -
SCHOOL HAPPENINGS--1974--LUNENBURG SCHOOLS
PERSONNEL CHANGES:
1974, in Lunenburg Schools, was a year of new faces and several personnel changes. In April,
Carolyn Simsarian and John Ludwig were elected to the School Committee and John Davis was elected
Chairman of that group. Roger Raboin decided not to run for a second term in order to devote more
time to his own business. In his three years on the School Committee, Mr. Raboin took a deep inter-
est in all facets of the school system. His many contributions and his high level of commitment,
particularly in the time he devoted to his school committee functions will be sorely missed.
Several important administrative appointments were also made last Spring. Following the
retirement of Dick Dustin, Paul Ralston of Morristown, Vermont, was appointed as the new principal
of the Thomas Passios Elementary School. Mr. Ralston brought with him the recent experience of
four years as principal of an innovative elementary program in Morrisville. In his short tenure in
-95-
===PAGE 98===
Lunenburg, Mr. Ralston has already demonstrated his abilities as a capable and knowledgeable
elementary school administrator.
In June, Richard Bishop and Thomas Starr, two middle school teachers, were appointed as admin-
istrative assistants to Mr. Barney at the Middle School. Filling a vacancy created by the resig-
nation of Robert Antonucci, who moved to Leominster as Assistant Superintendent of Schools, both
administrative assistants devote one-half of their time to administrative responsibilities and the
other half of their schedule to teaching responsibilities. This new administrative arrangement,
which was designed to provide qualified Lunenburg teachers with an opportunity for administrative
experience, has been working quite smoothly to date.
In other personnel actions, Jack White, Middle School Guidance Counselor, and Linda Howard,
Elementary School Guidance Counselor, both resigned to accept positions in other school systems--
positions which offered them professional advancement opportunities. Both counselors made signif-
icant contributions to Lunenburg Schools during their tenure in our school system.
In August, Bob Okerman replaced a capable Ray Lemieux as our Supervisor of Buildings &
Grounds. A talented "master of all trades", Mr. Okerman very quickly learned his new role and has
brought the school custodial and maintenance operations to new heights of efficiency resulting in
considerable savings to the Town.
And finally, Mrs. Doris Lahti, Head Town and School Nurse for the past twenty-one years,
announced her intention to retire in January, 1975. A tireless and dedicated worker, Mrs. Lahti is
a unique person who has contributed greatly to Lunenburg Schools and Lunenburg families over the
years. All staff members of the Lunenburg School System wish Doris much happiness in her retire-
ment.
CHAPTER 766 - THE NEW SPECIAL EDUCATION LAW:
In September, new regulations required Lunenburg to provide expanded educational services to
youngsters, aged 3 to 21, with special needs. The law also required local school systems to pick
up certain special education costs which were previously assumed directly by the State. Although
not providing for any new tax revenue to fund the new law, the State plans to reimburse local
cities and towns for a portion of their special education costs by use of new reimbursement
formulas. Mr. Burgess in his report provides details concerning our first year experiences with
the new law and its regulations.
Proceeding cautiously, Lunenburg provided for a $33,157. increase in its special education
budget last year to cover Chapter 766, a sum which included the hiring of one additional special
needs teacher. Because of the lack of inadequate special needs facilities in the elementary
school, voters in a special town meeting last Fall authorized the School Committee to spend
$48,000. in "up-front" Chapter 766 State Funds to construct a special needs resource classroom
addition to the Passios School. This addition, which is scheduled to be completed by September,
1975, will include two speech therapy rooms, a combined guidance/core evaluation team room, and
two distinct classroom areas for small groups of special needs students. Lunenburg is indeed for-
tunate to have such an addition approved and available for use in the near future.
Finally, our initial experiences with the law indicate a strong need for some increased staff-
ing if we are to meet the new demands being placed upon our present personnel. We purposely
delayed hiring extra special education staff last year since we wanted to gain experience with the
law and its detailed regulations. We now find that in the long run it may be more cost effective
to add selected staff in certain areas like speech and elementary guidance (e. g. psychological
testing) then to contract required services out to costly private clinics or consultants.
OTHER DEVELOPMENTS:
In June, 158 seniors graduated from Lunenburg High School with the outdoor graduation cere-
monies again blessed with outstanding weather. An analysis of the immediate plans of the graduates
revealed that 33% planned to attend four-year colleges, 20% planned to attend two-year colleges,
7% planned to attend vocational/technical schools, 5% planned to enter the military, and 35%
planned to go directly to work.

CONCLUSION:
In this my Fifth Annual Report as Superintendent of Schools, I would like to thank school
committee members, fellow administrators, teachers, students, and all members of the non-teaching
staff for their help and support during the past year. Special thanks must go to Burt Goodrich,
my assistant, and Mrs. Ginny Welch, my dedicated secretary--two people whose tremendous value to
Lunenburg Schools should not go unnoticed.
It is the united effort of all Lunenburg School staff members, however, that continues to keep
the Lunenburg School System "out front". In order to maintain our current high standing, we have
to constantly analyze our strengths and short-comings, be open to and accept criticism, and seek
improvement wherever necessary. This, I feel, we are prepared to do.
And finally, the continued support and encouragement of the community is critical to the main-
tenance of a quality educational program. As mentioned previously, the expectations a community
has for its schools are of critical importance. Traditionally, Lunenburg citizens have set high
(and realistic) standards for their schools. These citizens have a history of electing extremely
capable people to serve on their school committees. They have also enthusiastically and consist-
ently supported the efforts and progress of education locally over the years. There is no reason
to suspect that these same high expectations for our local schools are still not held by the
public even in times when sensible fiscal restraint is necessary and needed when formulating a
school budget or any budget for that matter.
Respectfully submitted,
DR. WILLIAM R. HOLLAND
Superintendent of Schools

REPORT OF THE ADMINISTRATIVE ASSISTANT
This report includes a sample of Fiscal Year
1974 programs and projects. Some have been chosen
because of their uniqueness, some because of the ex-
tensive effort and time devoted to them, and still
others because of their overall impact on the total
school system and community. An attempt has been
made to highlight some different and quite varied
facets of the school complex.
BURTON E. GOODRICH, JR.
Administrative Assistant
TRANSPORTATION:
Each child in Lunenburg, living a mile or more
from school, is transported daily to classes--a sim-
ple statement of fact, one often taken for granted.
Yet, when considered in total, what did this really mean to the Town in FY-74? Specifically, it
meant that each school day, twenty buses, covering forty separate routes, travelled 726 miles to
carry 1862 kindergarten through grade twelve pupils (approximately 85% of the total school system
enrollment). In the course of the 1973-74 school year, 130,680 miles were logged for regular
transportation!
Student participation in academic field trips and athletic events consumed another 8000 miles
as pupils travelled to 238 events. And, special needs children being transported within the town
and out-of-town to such locations as Fitchburg, Leominster, and Worcester, required approximately
20,000 miles.
Total cost for all transportation services was $158,695.; approximately 76% of this cost was
reimbursed by the State to Lunenburg. Thus, the net cost of transportation was $38,500. in FY-74.
The very reasonable bids submitted by local contractors during the past several years for
transportation is responsible for keeping this cost in line. By coupling the cost factor with the
important human elements of proper pupil deportment, parent cooperation, and excellent bus
contractor and driver service, Lunenburg got superior "mileage" from its transportation service in
1974.
FEDERAL GOVERNMENT- GRANTS:
Besides the Federal and State reimbursements that are routinely received by the Town for edu-
cation, Lunenburg acquired four special Federal Grants for FY-74. The total amount for these pro-
grams was $30,347. and was dispursed as follows:
(1) $25, 386. was approved from Title I of P. L. 89-10 of the Elementary and Secondary
Education Act (ESEA), for providing the salaries of a Remedial Arithmetic teacher, a
Remedial Reading teacher, supplies, equipment and an independent evaluation. Both
remedial teachers worked with pupils in the elementary schools who were behind their
normal grade level of achievement in arithmetic and/or reading. Receipt of the grant
marked Lunenburg's 9th year of participation in Title I programs.
(2) $4961. was approved from Title III of Public Low 864 of the National Defense Educa-
tion Act (NDEA). These funds represented reimbursement to the Town of Lunenburg for
50% of the cost of instructional equipment and materials purchased for use in three
areas-- (a) Middle School Individualized Math ($1747.); (b) High School Science
($2219.), and (c) High School Industrial Arts ($995.).
-98-
===PAGE 101===

CAREER EDUCATION:
Career Education received considerable attention in FY-74 as various staff members were in-
volved in curriculum analysis, program design and implementation. Four important accomplishments
were made in this realm during the past year:
1. Career Education was closely studied with the aim of developing and articulating a total
program of awareness, exploration and skill training experiences. A Career Development Curriculum
was written to identify programs currently in existence and to provide a foundation from which
future plans could be made for redesigning curriculum, adding new programs and filling gaps that
exist.
2. The receipt of approximately $70,000. in Federal money in FY-73 made possible the major
remodeling of a storage area in the High School into graphic arts laboratories and darkroom com-
plete with equipment. In the Fall of 1974, students began studies in graphic arts. Through the
Business Education Department, pupils could learn about graphic arts input--the preparation of
materials to be printed. And, through the Industrial Arts Department, pupils could learn about
graphic arts output--the printing of materials.
This first year saw interest and participation very high as sixty-six boys and girls elected
courses in graphic arts. Along with receiving classroom instruction in awareness, exploration and
skill training, these pupils performed valuable printing services--they handled just about all of
the school system's printing needs!
3. A new career awareness/exploration program was developed for all eighth grade boys and
girls. It replaced the "traditional" program that placed boys in industrial arts and girls in
home economics for a full year of study.
The new program stressed awareness and exploration of various career fields toward which stu-
dents could pursue studies at Lunenburg High School or Monty Tech. Boys and girls were mixed
-99-
===PAGE 102===
evenly in classes and meet for one double period per week for the entire school year. For approxi-
mately eighteen weeks, pupils attended industrial arts laboratories where they spent two to three
classes becoming familiar with each of eight career fields:
MATERIAL TRANSFORMATION: (1) Wood - students were involved in a production line for the con-

the industry--rotational moulding, injection moulding, forming and joining plastic. The
students worked on a small project of their choice in any of the several areas offered.

such as quick breads and cookies, custards, egg dishes were taught. (6) FOOD TODAY - pupils stud-
ied the four basic nutrition groups, as well as, health foods, gardening, and food consumer educa-
tion.
In essence, this 8th grade awareness/exploration program provided a change of pace from Middle
School experiences in the study of Home Economics and Industrial Arts. It provided all 8th grade
pupils (boys and girls, college and non-college) with the same familiarity of major career fields.
And, most importantly, provided students with knowledge to help them decide on courses of study at
Lunenburg High School or Montachusett Regional Vocational/Technical School.
-100-
===PAGE 103===
4. Because the business world, parents and the community are looking to the schools to do more
preparation in introducing students to the world of work, a Career Exploration Program was begun in
FY-74 at Monty Tech to help expose seventh through twelfth grade students to six different career
clusters.
The program was designed to allow students
an opportunity to explore several related
career areas within a cluster grouping--hope-
fully, this assisted the student in making an
eventual career selection and/or lead the stu-
dent into a specific course in the Job Entry
Skill category. Lunenburg students selected
clusters in automotive, building trades, food
trades, electronics, community services,
graphic arts and data processing. Each cluster
meet one or two days per week from 3 to 5 P. M.
for fifteen weeks.
IN-SERVICE COURSES:
Four in-service courses were held in Lun-
enburg for teachers and administrators during
FY-74 in which approximately sixty-five Lunen-
burg staff members as well as other school dis-
trict personnel were represented.
An ART WORKSHOP was conducted by Mrs Nelde
Drumm, Art Supervisor; Mrs. Margaret Baratta,
Elementary School Art teacher; and Mrs. Marilyn
Tanguay, Middle School Art teacher. This course
was designed to help teachers understand child
EIGHTH GRADE CAREER AWARENESS/
growth through art education, understand art
EXPLORATION PROGRAM
processes and techniques and activities recom-
mended for children, as well as having aimed at
other objectives for the classroom teacher. Participants in the course worked with such media as
filmstrips, crayon, tempera and watercolor, clay, plaster, holiday art, graphics, paper sculpture,
paper mache, and lettering techniques.
IMPLEMENTATION OF PROGRAMMABLE CALCULATORS IN THE MATH AND SCIENCE CURRICULA was sponsored by
the High School Science Department with an instructor from Wange Laboratories. The course was
designed to help math and science teachers in Grades 5 through 12 integrate programmable calculators
into existing curriculum by providing a supplementary teaching tool for reinforcing concepts, chal-
lenging above average students, and remotivating low achieving students. The first six sessions
dealt with the machine operations and basic programming techniques including branching, looping,
and decision-making. Formulas from math and science were selected. The second six sessions were
designed to utilize the participants programming knowledge of developing a conceptually based
"Calculator Assisted Learning Library" of his own.
An "INSIDE/OUT" COURSE was taught by John White (former Turkey Hill Middle School guidance
counselor) under the auspices of the Massachusetts College of Pharmacy in both Spring and Fall
semesters. Inside/Out is a television series available on video tapes or film for use in classrooms
that engages the minds and feelings of 8 to 12 year olds by presenting realistic situations in dra-
matic or documentary formats. The series treats such themes as responsibility, taking dares, getting
even, prejudice, competition, bullying, death of a loved one, the pain of moving away and sibling
rivalry. Each program deals with a single theme; each program explores the entire range of possible
attitudes and feelings about its topic.
The Inside/Out program nicely dovetailed with Lunenburg's developing Health Education Program
in which is stressed the basics of physical and mental health, as well as respect for self and
others.

REPORT OF HIGH SCHOOL PRINCIPAL
SCHOOL EVALUATION AND ACADEMIC ACCREDITATION:
In 1969, Lunenburg High School was recommended
and voted for continued membership and full ten-year
accreditation in the New England Association of
Schools and Colleges as a result of a self-evaluation
by its faculty and a report of a visiting committee
of over twenty educators from high schools and
colleges throughout New England.
The fifty-page report consisted of commendations
and recommendations regarding every phase of the
high school program and its facilities. The purpose
of the report was to help school officials as they
look to further improve the quality of secondary
education in Lunenburg.
In order to maintain full accreditation, the
RICHARD J. CAREY
Commission requires that schools file progress
Principal
reports at two and five-year intervals following the
evaluation. The progress reports must indicate the
disposition of each recommendation according to the
following categories: (1) completed, (2) in progress,
(3) planned for the future, (4) rejected as invalid
or impractical, and (5) no action.
The two and five-year reports have been sub-
mitted by the high school principal with the cooper-
ation of department heads and supervisors with the
five-year report being submitted during 1974. In
the five-year report, 82% of the two hundred seventy-
five recommendations had been satisfactorily imple-
mented, including a work/study program, curriculum
changes, graphic communication/technology program,
comprehensive health education program, employment
of full-time audio-visual coordinator, integration
with vocational/technical school district, efforts
to upgrade the facilities, etc.
On September 29th and 30th, 1974, the progress
report for Lunenburg High School was officially
accepted by the Commission and accreditation was ex-
tended to continue through the ten-year period until
PHILIP M. CROUSE
the next decennial evaluation of Lunenburg High
Assistant Principal
School which must be conducted in 1979.
-102-
===PAGE 105===
Each year, three to four members of the high school staff are released to take part in the
Commission's efforts to evaluate other public high schools in New England. This is allowed in
order that our own professionals will be aware of acceptable practices currently taking place in
the field of education.
FACULTY/ADMINISTRATION COMMITTEES:
There are presently approximately sixty members of the professional staff of Lunenburg High
School. In order to determine the effectiveness of various programs and to implement others it
has become necessary to establish many faculty/administration committees which eventually report
to the faculty as a whole. Their recommendations are then challenged for acceptance or rejection.
Among the committees formulated, there is one which is re-examining our graduation require-
ments. These are being studied because of the increase in independent study courses, pass/fail
courses, teacher aides and assistants, courses less than a full year, work/study credits, and
other program alternatives introduced in recent years.
Another committee has been formed to formulate specific goals, policies, procedures, guide-
lines, requirements, etc., for all out-of-classroom experiences for which academic credit is
granted.
Finally, an ad hoc committee has been formed to re-examine the policies regarding teacher
responsibilities toward fellow colleagues, students and the school. This group has examined the
important area of guidance policies and the role of the regular classroom teacher in counseling
procedures and directions.
ASSESSMENT TESTS:
Lunenburg High School took part in a
state assessment program in 1974 which was a
goal adopted by the Massachusetts Board of
Education. Tests were administered to a ran-
dom sampling of seventeen-year old boys and
girls throughout the State to measure reading,
mathematics, and decision-making skills and
occupational knowledge and attitude.
The Assessment Program serves four
to
goals: (1) to provide the citizenry with
accurate and useful information about the
quality of education in Mass.; (2) to pro-
vide state level decision-makers with informa-
tion SO that they may make better informed
30
JIMENBUR
policy decisions; (3) to provide school
committees and administrators with information
as an aid in setting their own objectives;
(4) to make available state-wide assessment
procedures so that local school districts can
use them in developing their own assessment
practices.
In CONCLUSION, I would like to thank
fellow administrators, especially my very
capable assistant, Mr. Philip Crouse;teachers,
school committee members and members of the
non-teaching staff, for their dedicated
support and outstanding service during the
past year.

REPORT OF DIRECTOR OF GUIDANCE
AND SPECIAL SERVICES
FIRST YEAR OF CHAPTER 766: A PROGRESS REPORT
Chapter 766 burst upon the Lunenburg Schools
this year as it did for all Massachusetts school
systems. We have constantly been evaluating its
impact on the services we can and must provide to
children with special needs. (A "child with
special needs" is defined as one who must have
special help in order to progress effectively in
the regular education program.)
JOHN I. BURGESS
From our observations SO far, there's good
Director of Guidance
news and bad news. The good news is that the law
& Special Services
and its regulations do help formalize, make
accountable, and provide needed services to chil-
dren. The intent of the law is being carried out, although the process and paperwork is slower
than originally foreseen. The bad news is that such accountability spotlights glaringly the need
for more adequate staffing and programs for children whose special needs have not been met by
public schools before.
I'll briefly mention three selected areas of
real progress to date, to indicate some of the
3123456789
scope of the law and its applications.
(1) PARENT INVOLVEMENT - The Core Evalua-
tion Team (CET) process by which a child is
determined to have a special need emphasizes
the role and rights of the parent and child in
determining what services are needed. We con-
sider the parents' involvement necessary in
helping us understand the learning style and
needs of their children, and we ask parents to
meet with us several times in the course of
the CET process as part of the evaluation team.
This process has brought us closer and we feel
the parents closer to viewing a child's educa-
tion as a truly cooperative venture of school,
parent, and child.
(2) RESOURCE ROOM - At a special town
meeting this fiscal year, a multi-purpose
resource room was approved to be added to the
Passios Elementary School. This room will
help provide more services and more efficient
use of staff and materials than we had. The
room will be financed by money advanced by the
SUBSTANTIALLY SEPARATE CLASS: (l-r)
State for the first year of Chapter 766. Even
Steven Cecchini; Mathew Fortune;
so, the Passios School is, and still will be
Mrs. Barbara Curtis, teacher; Steven
using every bit of space for regular and
Soucy. (Not appearing in picture are
special education.
Thomas Padula and Jamie Pietluck)
(3) SPECIAL NEEDS COLLABORATIVE - The Special Needs Educational Collaborative (SNEC) has
been formed with our neighboring towns (Ashby, Townsend, Pepperell, Groton, Dunstable, Ayer, Shir-
ley, Tyngsboro, and Lunenburg) to help provide and plan for those kinds of very special needs that
might involve only one child in each town. Many such services can be best provided in a small
classroom situation, and the most sensible and least expensive way is to get together with our
-104-
===PAGE 107===
neighboring towns to share such programs. Other benefits have been increased sharing of exper-
iences and staff in-service training, and eligibility for state/federal funding that small towns
could not expect to receive singularly.
In the long run, given patience, common sense, and some financial restraint by all parties
concerned with Chapter 766, this law will provide us with the experience and expertise to provide
better services to all children through individualized, flexible learning experiences, and the
development of programs designed to identify early and prevent learning problems.
Respectfully submitted,
JOHN I. BURGESS
Director of Guidance & Special Services
REPORT OF MIDDLE SCHOOL PRINCIPAL
FLOYD F. BARNEY
Principal
RICHARD J. BISHOP
Administrative Assistant
The Middle School administration, faculty,
secretaries, custodians, cafeteria ladies, and
other personnel greeted 564 students on open-
ing day of the 1974-75 school year. Our pres-
ent enrollment now stands at 569.
The Middle School encompasses grades 5,
6, and 7, with grade 7 leading the way with a
student enrollment of 206. As a point of
interest, the girl-boy ratio in grade 7 is
rather unusual this year because we have
approximately 40 more boys than girls at this
grade level.
Students on opening day saw many new
faces due to a large turn-over in faculty mem-
bers this year. The turn-over in faculty was
a result of transfers within the system, peo-
ple promoted to higher positions, husbands
being transferred to other states, expectant
THOMAS STARR
mothers and many other reasons.
Administrative Assistant
-105-
===PAGE 108===
It is impossible in a limited report to go into great detail on every phase of our Middle
School curriculum or other parts of our program. Hence, we will briefly go over some new changes
and report on some of our on-going programs.
PERSONNEL CHANGES:
Mr. Starr and Mr. Bishop are the new Administrative Assistants in the Middle School. These
new positions were created as a result of our previous assistant principal, Mr. Antonucci, accept-
ing the position of Assistant Superintendent in the Leominster School System. Mr. Starr and Mr.
Bishop teach Math in grade 7 on a half-time basis and devote the rest of their time to administra-
tive duties.
Mrs. Willow Shire is the new Guidance Counselor in the Middle School, replacing Mr. John White
who accepted a position at Monty Tech. Much of Mrs. Shire's time is now devoted to Chapter 766, a
new state law that deals with children in need of special services. Although the intent of the
new mandated law is good, the new paper work and time commitments required create a monumental
task. Hence, other guidance programs have not been as productive as we anticipated for the year.
RESOURCE ROOM:
With the advent of Chapter 766, we have created a resource room that works with children in
need of special services. Miss Sharon Finneron, who taught last year's grade 5 transitional class
and is trained in special education, coordinates this area. In practice, students who indicate a
need for supportive help may be referred to the resource area for individual help.
In addition to Miss Finneron, we have three other specialists assigned to the resource room
on a half-time basis. Mr. Alan Wilson works in speech therapy, Mrs. Pauline Celona is an adjust-
ment counselor, and Mr. Walter Bossidy works with children who have a learning disability.
TRANSITIONAL CLASSES:
We have two transitional classes in the Middle School that have proven to be very worthwhile
for some students. Miss Mary Bouchard is the transitional class teacher in grade 5 and Mr. Robert
Saudelli works with the transitional group in grade 6.
A transitional class does not work with children with severe problems, but deals with children
who need more confidence or reinforcement in the basic skills. The classes are smaller than the
regular classes which enables the teacher to spend more time with a student on an individual basis.
The key to the transitional class is flexibility. For example, the transitional teachers are not
locked into a block of time or restricted to a 45-minute Language Arts class. Miss Bouchard and
Mr. Saudelli assess what skills are in need of reinforcement and have the opportunity to devcte
more time in areas where students need specialized assistance.
The students in our transitional classes are not isolated from the rest of the school program
such as Science, Social Studies, Industrial Arts, Homemaking, Physical Education, Art or Music.
They simply are given more individualized help in the areas of Language Arts and Mathematics with
the hope of improving their basic skills and increasing their self-confidence.
OPEN HOUSE:
Again we conducted an Open House on two different days this year. The response from the par-
ents was very gratifying.
Our open house format is not to be confused with Parent-Teacher Conferences which are held
later session. in the year. Open House gives the parents the opportunity to visit any or all classes in
We do not have any special programs during Open House because we want the parents to actually
"get the feel" of how classes are usually conducted.

===PAGE 109===

REPORT OF ELEMENTARY SCHOOL PRINCIPAL
The 1974-75 school year began with an enrollment
of seven hundred seventy-four students. This reflected
a decrease of approximately thirty children from last
year's enrollment figures. The September 1974 distri-
bution of students reflected a substantial increase
in class sizes at the first grade level. To reduce
the teacher-pupil ratio and thus provide a more con-
centrated effort at the first grade level, it was
decided to add an additional class. Since one fourth
grade position had been eliminated, this was accom-
plished without the addition of extra staff.
As a result of these changes, our class sizes
PAUL E. RALSTON
have moved closer to a desired twenty-five pupil
Principal
average. At the present time, there is one transi-
tional class at each grade level for grades one
through four. These classes average about sixteen
pupils. Averages for other classes are as follows:
first grade, twenty-four; second grade, twenty-six;
third grade, twenty-four; and fourth grade, thirty.
Our present kindergarten enrollment is one hundred
twenty-nine pupils.
LIBRARY EXPANSION:
Our space needs in the elementary schools remain
critical. To accommodate an additional first grade
class, it was necessary to relocate our library in a
converted supply closet. As soon as construction of
the special needs area is completed, the storage
closet currently used by the speech therapist will be
vacated. As this is adjacent to the present library,
the partition will be removed, thus doubling the size
of this area. Hopefully, this work will be completed
by next September.
ROBERT FINNERON
Assistant Principal
Our long-range plans for this area would include
the development of a school/parent resource center.
This would provide both a resource for children, as
well as an adult section with books and materials dealing with such topics as child adoption, sex
education, divorce and children, values and feelings.
PLAYGROUND IMPROVEMENTS:
At the present time a committee of parents and teachers are developing a land-use plan for
the areas surrounding our schools. The goal of this group is to establish learning areas outside
of the classroom. Included in the plan will be a nature trail as well as creative play areas.
With the realization that children learn best through their experiences ,we feel that opportunities
should be available, both in and outside of the classroom, for participatory learning.
CURRICULUM ADOPTIONS:
Social Studies - Data Bank in the Elementary Schools - For the past two years, a group of
third and fourth grade teachers have been using the Holt "Data Bank" Social Studies program. (Two
years in the fourth grade; one year in the third grade.)
This program attempts to overcome two major areas of concern in social studies textbooks.
These areas are, "motivation" and "reading problems". Data Bank provides a wide variety of teaching
-108-
===PAGE 111===
materials. It recognizes that varied resources by themselves stimulate the learner and heighten
interest. With these rich resources at hand, teachers are free to utilize a wide range of teaching
techniques, from role-playing to reading assignments.
The Data Bank System uses reading as one important means, but not the only means by which
knowledge and understanding is gained. The pupils read a text, but they also learn through the
use of film, recordings, fold-out cards, games, puzzles, and a variety of classroom strategies
devised to help each pupil master the material being presented. This variety of experience bene-
fits all pupils, offering the slower student alternate modes of learning and challenging the abler
student to more than read a text and recall facts.
After two years of study, Data Bank will
be expanded next year to include all third and
fourth grades. At the third grade level, the
topic for study is Inquiring About Cities:
Studies in Geography and Economics. For the
fourth grades, the topic is Inquiring About
Cultures: Studies in Anthropology and
Sociology. With this adoption, the Lunenburg
Schools will be using the Holt "Data Bank"
Social Studies Program for all pupils in grades
three through six.
Mathematics - This has been the second
year of our pilot program in I. M. S. The
Individualized Mathematics System was used
this year in two second, three third, and four
fourth grades. The I. M. S. program provides
maximum flexibility for teachers and pupils,
and does not penalize pupils if they are not
proficient in reading. Covering eleven topics:
numeration, addition, subtraction, multiplica-
tion, division, fractions, mixed operations,
money, time, measurement and geometry, there
is a carefully arranged sequence of skills to
be learned. The skills are organized by level
ARITHMETIC FACTS ARE TAUGHT BY Mrs.
of difficulty and each skill is incorporated
Gladys H. Van Horne, teacher of
into a separate skill folder. The skill
Remedial Arithmetic, hired by Fed-

eral Funds of the Title I, Elemen-
styles to satisfy the needs of all pupils.
tary & Secondary Education Act.
In this, the second year of the pilot program, the I. M. S. Management Guide was adapted to
provide for more group work. This enabled our teachers to place greater emphasis on basic facts
and operations and pinpoint individual trouble spots along the way. The reaction of pupils and
teachers alike -has been most favorable. The lack of drill and reinforcement noted in the first
year has been remedied by more frequent "seminars" (group work) scheduled this year.
As a result of the two-year pilot program, it has been decided to adopt I. M. S. as the math-
ematics system for grades three and four as of September 1975.
CO-CURRICULAR PROGRAMS:
During the course of the 1974-75 school year, a series of safety programs has been presented
in our schools. These programs have involved our local fire department, as well as, the police
department. The students in grades three and four have participated in a bicycle safety program
which encompassed voluntary bicycle inspections, safety lectures and a bicycle rodeo. Plans have
been made to follow this up each year with a safety week devoted to educating the children in the
areas of bicycle, fire, bus, and pedestrian safety habits.
A visiting arts and crafts program was also initiated in the elementary grades this year. Our
intent is to expose the children to a variety of children's theatre and crafts demonstrations as
well as educational field trips and lectures. I would like to thank the P-TO for their financial
-109-
===PAGE 112===
assistance, as well as Mr. Einard
Warpula and Mrs. Mary-Jane Peterson

have been visits to such places as
local businesses, a planetarium, and
the Worcester Science Museum. We
have also hosted several assemblies
featuring puppet shows, lecture/
demonstrations, and craft displays.

===PAGE 120===
Retirements
EFFECTIVE AUGUST 29, 1974 - MR. ALFRED A, MORIN
"Fred", as he was known to his co-workers, began as a Junior Custodian in the Passios Elemen-
tary School on October 5, 1970. Although his tenure with the school system was short, he gave
conscientious and commendable service in the performance of his duties as one of the "housekeepers"
of the Passios School.
EFFECTIVE AUGUST 31, 1974 - MR. RICHARD H. DUSTIN
"Dick" was elected by the Lunenburg School Committee on
July 30, 1962, as Assistant Principal of the Passios Elemen-
tary School, with duties to begin August 20, 1962. Prior to
Lunenburg, he had had previous experience of eighteen years--
2 years in Wells, Maine; 3 years in Plainfield, Mass. and 13
years (the last 6 as Supervising Principal) in Middlefield,
Conn. Dick served in the U. S. Army from 1941 - 1946 with the
Signal Corps with rank of Major in charge of the Signal Center
in New Delhi, India, for the China/Burma/India theater of
operation.
On June 20, 1966, the Lunenburg School Committee elected
Dick to the position of Principal, replacing Tom Passios who
had resigned to join the Department of Education. He served
in this capacity until his retirement effective August 31,
1974. In a letter sent to Dick on December 13, 1973, the
Lunenburg School Committee expressed their sincere apprecia-
tion for his positive leadership during his twelve years in Lunenburg and commented on his contri-
butions to the growth and current quality of the elementary school.
On Sunday, November 3, 1974, the community tendered a reception for Dick in the Passios
Elementary School, at which time a brief program was conducted with gift presentations made by the
Lunenburg 4-H Club and the Reception Committee. As a result of one of the gifts, Dick has spent
his first months of retirement in Hawaii and has been the envy of all of us this past snow-filled
winter.
EFFECTIVE DECEMBER 31, 1974 - MRS. DORIS M. LAHTI
Doris began her duties as Assistant part-time School
Nurse on March 15, 1954 when increased enrollment made it
necessary to hire an additional school nurse. Over the years,
she has assumed the position of Supervising Nurse and at the
time of her retirement, there were two other nurses working
under her direction.
An Open House is being planned for Doris for Sunday,
March 2, 1975, at the Turkey Hill Middle School, at which
time, the community and school personnel may express its
profound thanks and appreciation to this dedicated and con-
scientious lady for her outstanding service to the Town and
Schools for the past twenty-one years.
-118-
===PAGE 121===
DEATHS DURING 1974
of former school employees
January 9, 1974 (in Philadelphia, Pennsylvania) - Miss BLANCHE E. LANCEY
Miss Lancey was born in Lunenburg, November 7, 1891, and was a teacher and principal in the
Lunenburg School System for forty-five years before her retirement. She was a graduate of Lunenburg
High School and the Fitchburg Normal School (now Fitchburg State College), as well as a piano
teacher for many years. She also taught in Hillsboro, NH; Saxton's River, VT; Townsend, MA; and
Ridgewood, NJ.
Blanche first began in Lunenburg in the old Center School building on November 9th, 1914 as
teacher of "A" intermediate. An additional room had to be opened at this level due to increased
enrollment. From September, 1926 to June, 1945, she was a teacher of Grade Four for three years,
Grade Five for two years; and Grade Six for fourteen years. From September 1945 to June 1953
(eight years), she continued as teacher of Grade Four, as well as assuming the duties of Principal
of the Center School. In September, 1953, Miss Lancey transferred to the new elementary school,
continuing her teaching duties at the Grade Four level. She remained in this position until her
retirement on June 30, 1958. Many residents and employees of the Town of Lunenburg were former
pupils of Miss Lancey and have many fond memories of their association with her during the years
she was a teacher In the Lunenburg Public Schools.
April 17, 1974 (in Fitchburg) - Mr. JOHN F. MUNRO
John was born in Fitchburg on October 20, 1916 and
attended schools there. He was an army veteran of World War
11 and served in the European Theater. He was awarded a
Purple Heart.
John first began as custodian in the Lunenburg School
System on December 16, 1953 when he was hired to fill a cus-
todial vacancy in the Center School where junior high school
grades were being housed. He remained in this building until
January 3, 1961, when he transferred to the Passios Elementary
School. On July 21, 1967, he transferred to the Senior High
School as a day man, replacing Mr. Al Balcom, who was retiring.
At the time of his death, John was a custodian at the Turkey
Hill Middle School.
Mr. Munro served the schools well with over twenty years
of dedicated and devoted service. John, though a quiet and
unassuming man, left his mark on the custodial operation and
his loss was felt by all.

===PAGE 122===
nurse
REPORT
OF
as submitted by:
school nurse
DORIS M. LAHTI, R. N.
Supervising Nurse
January 1, 1975 will see changes in the Nursing staff. Mrs. Janet Pace, who holds a Bachelors
Degree in Nursing, will be supervising the program, replacing Mrs. Doris Lahti who retired. Mrs.
Shirley Carpenter will be working full-time because Mrs. Gladys Barney is on indefinite leave-of-
absence. Mrs. Jean Hobbs will be rehired to be the part-time nurse; Mrs. Barbara Eley is the part-
time secretary.
The above registered nurses are employed by the Board of Health and perform the Visiting
Nursing as well as the School Nursing. Dr. John DeLeo is School Physician.
A total of 141 visits were made to parents of school children for physical defects, absence
from school, for Chapter 766 Program and follow-up on vision and hearing failures.
Parents are always welcome to the nurses' offices to discuss any problem their child might
have, or to call the nurses by phone.
Mrs. Barney and Mrs. Carpenter both attended the workshop on the Chapter 766 Program. This
course for continuing education consisted of six four-hour sessions.
The Nashoba Associated Boards of Health offer the following programs in the schools--each pro-
gram requires signed permission from parents with the exception of the Educational Dental Program:
German Measles (Rubella) and Mumps Vaccine to certain grades.
Grade 1 only - Trivalent Oral Polio Series or Polio Booster for
those who have previously completed oral series Tine testing.
Tetanus and Diphtheria Booster.
Grades 2, 5, and 8 - Dental Fluoride Program given by Dental
Hygienist.
Grade 11 - Tetanus and Diphtheria Booster and Smallpox
Revaccination.
The above programs are well accepted by parents thus keeping Lunenburg Schools immunization
rate at a high level.
Vision and Hearing Testing is done annually on all students, Kindergarten through Grade 12.
Notices of failures are mailed home to parents with a doctor's form attached. This form must be
returned to the nurse's office after the child has been seen by an eye or ear doctor in order for
the school to follow the doctor's recommendations
The nurses appreciate very much the help of the administrators and school personnel in making
the School Health Program successful.
In closing, I wish to say I shall never forget the loyalty, cooperation and understanding that
was given to me by the administrators, the entire school personnel, parents, and students over the
last twenty years.

===PAGE 125===
School Lunch Program Report
by MARJORIE H. STILLMAN
School Lunch Supervisor
A sizable undertaking in this department was the remodeling of the T. C. Passios Elementary
School and Lunenburg High School kitchens. This was realized through 75% funding by the State
Department of Food Services. At the Passios School, a double convection oven, two-burner hot-top
stove, two-compartment steamer, three-section refrigerator, baking table and bins, pot washer,
proofer and small utensils were purchased. At the High School, a double convection oven, three-
compartment sink with pot washer, two-burner hot-top stove, and small utensils were acquired. At
both schools, walk-in freezers, surplus property donated from the State's phased-out food
assistance program, were installed. Thus, both kitchens were modernized and made more efficient
through local funds of $4266. and State funds of $12,798.

pressures of inflation. Our special milk program--this is individual sales of milk to go with bag
lunch--is up as is free milk. Our reimbursement is relative to participation.
